\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha<1
, we can take in
(
6.20
) the order of the difference in
t
to be one,
m=1
. And we fix
some integer
k>\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha})

as the order of the difference in the space variables
z_{n}
. To apply the
mentioned lemma we choose the exponent
a
as
a=\{\theta\}
and then the
exponent
b
is defined from the relation

\frac{\{\theta\}}{\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha}+\frac{b}{\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha})}=1,

that is

b=\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)\frac{\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha}{\theta+\theta\alpha}\frac{\theta\alpha}{\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha}=\sigma_{n}\alpha.

Thus we have on the ground of (
6.20
) (
h\in R^{N_{n}}
)

\left|\delta_{h,z_{n}}^{k}\left[u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)-u_{\tau}^{([\theta])}(x,0)\right]\right|\leq

\leq C\left(\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha)}+\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{z_{n},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha}))}\right)t^{\{\theta\}}|h|^{\sigma_{n}\alpha}

or, dividing both parts by
t^{\{\theta\}}
,

\left|\delta_{h,z_{n}}^{k}I_{1}\right|\leq C\left(\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha)}+\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{z_{n},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha}))}\right)|h|^{\sigma_{n}\alpha}

that is

\left\langle I_{1}\right\rangle_{z_{n},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{n}\alpha)}\leq C\left(\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha)}+\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{z_{n},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha}))}\right).

(6.37)

Estimate now the seminorm of the integral
I_{2}
in (
6.35
). Let

h\in R^{N_{n}}
be fixed. Suppose first
that
t>|h|^{\sigma_{n}/\theta}
and decompose the integral in
I_{2}
into
two parts as follows

I_{2}={\int\limits_{t-|h|^{\sigma_{n}/\theta}}^{t}}\frac{\left[u_{\tau}^{([\theta])}(x,\tau)-u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)\right]d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{1+\{\theta\}}}+

+{\int\limits_{0}^{t-|h|^{\sigma_{n}/\theta}}}\frac{\left[u_{\tau}^{([\theta])}(x,\tau)-u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)\right]d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{1+\{\theta\}}}\equiv J_{1}+J_{2}.

Estimate separately the finite differences
\delta_{h,z_{n}}^{k}J_{1}
and

\delta_{h,z_{n}}^{k}J_{2}
,
k>\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha})
. On the ground of the Hölder property of

u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)
in
t
we have for
J_{1}

|\delta_{h,z_{n}}^{k}J_{1}|\leq C\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha)}{\int\limits_{t-|h|^{\sigma_{n}/\theta}}^{t}}\frac{(t-\tau)^{\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha}d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{1+\{\theta\}}}=

=C\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha)}\left(|h|^{\sigma_{n}/\theta}\right)^{\theta\alpha}=C\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha)}|h|^{\sigma_{n}\alpha}.

(6.38)

Use now the Hölder property of
u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)
in
z_{n}
to
obtain

|\delta_{h,z_{n}}^{k}J_{2}|\leq C\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{z_{n},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha}))}|h|^{\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha})}{\int\limits_{0}^{t-|h|^{\sigma_{n}/\theta}}}\frac{d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{1+\{\theta\}}}=

=C\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{z_{n},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha}))}|h|^{\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha})}\left(|h|^{\frac{\sigma_{n}}{\theta}}\right)^{-\{\theta\}}=

=C\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{z_{n},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha}))}|h|^{\sigma_{n}\alpha}.

(6.39)

If now
t\leq|h|^{\sigma_{n}/\theta}
, then analogously to the estimate for

\delta_{h,z_{n}}^{k}J_{1}
we have for
\delta_{h,z_{n}}^{k}I_{2}

|\delta_{h,z_{n}}^{k}I_{2}|\leq C\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha)}{\int\limits_{0}^{|h|^{\sigma_{n}/\theta}}}\frac{(t-\tau)^{\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha}d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{1+\{\theta\}}}=C\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha)}|h|^{\sigma_{n}\alpha}.

(6.40)

Collecting estimates (
6.38
) - (
6.40
), we see that

\left\langle I_{2}\right\rangle_{z_{n},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{n}\alpha)}\leq C\left(\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha)}+\left\langle u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right\rangle_{z_{n},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{n}(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{[\theta]}{\theta+\theta\alpha}))}\right).

(6.41)

The assertion of the proposition and estimate (
6.33
) follow now from
(
6.34
), (
6.36
), (
6.37
) and (
6.41
).

We have also some more general assertion.

Theorem 6.9

Let
\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha<1
and let a noninteger
\mu\in(0,\theta+\theta\alpha)
so that it can be represented as
\mu=(1-\omega)\theta(1+\alpha)
,
\omega\in(0,1)
.

The operator
D_{\ast t}^{\mu}
is a bonded linear operator from

C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
to

C^{\omega\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\omega\theta(1+\alpha)}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
that is for
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(R_{T}^{N})

|D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\omega\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\omega\theta(1+\alpha))}\leq C(N,\omega,\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)|u|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{\left(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha\right)}.

(6.42)

Proof.
The theorem is a direct consequence of the previous Proposition. It is enough
to note that

\theta(1+\alpha)=\mu(1+\beta),\quad\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)=\overline{\rho}(1+\beta),\quad\beta\equiv\frac{1}{1-\omega}-1=\frac{\omega}{1-\omega}>0,

where

\beta\equiv\frac{1}{1-\omega}-1=\frac{\omega}{1-\omega}>0,\quad\rho_{k}\equiv(1-\omega)\sigma_{k}(1+\alpha).

Thus
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})=C^{\overline{\rho}(1+\beta),\mu+\mu\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
.
Consequently, the assertion of the theorem is obtained by the application of
Proposition
6.8
.

It was shown in the example after Theorem
2.1
that the restriction

\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha<1
can not be omitted in general. However this
restriction is a possible sufficient condition for the assertions of
Proposition
6.8
and Theorem
6.9
to be valid. As a conclusion of
this section we present here some another sufficient condition. It is that all
the permitted by a class derivatives in
t
vanish at
t=0
.

But first we prove an auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 6.10

Let a function
f(t)\in C^{\theta+\beta}([0,\infty))
, where

\theta
and
\theta+\beta
are nonintegers and let at
t=0
the function

f(t)
satisfy the condition

f_{t}^{(k)}(0)=0,\quad k=0,...,[\theta].

(6.43)

Then it’s fractional Caputo - Jrbashyan derivative
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f(t)
,
which was defined in (
1.4
), (
1.9
), coincides with the Marshaud
derivative(see
[
1
]
, section 5.4) that is it has also the
representation

D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f(t)=C(\theta,m){\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}}\frac{\delta_{-\tau,t}^{m}f(t)}{\tau^{1+\theta}}d\tau\equiv C(\theta,m)T_{\theta,m}(f).

(6.44)

Here
\delta_{-\tau,t}f(t)=f(t)-f(t-\tau)
,
\delta_{-\tau,t}^{n}f(t)=

\delta_{-\tau,t}\left(\delta_{-\tau,t}^{m-1}f(t)\right)
is a
backward difference of an arbitrary but fixed order
m>\theta
and the
function
f(t)
is extended by identical zero in the domain
t<0
.

Proof.
The proof reproduces the schema of reasonings from
[
1
]
, section 25.4,
Lemma 25.3 for the fractional Riess differentiation as it is applied in

[
1
]
to obtain representation (
1.12
). Namely, we compare the
Fourier transforms of the left and of the right hand sides of (
6.44
).

Note that zero extension of the function
f(t)
in the domain
t<0
belongs to
the space
C^{\theta+\beta}
in the case
\{\theta\}+\beta<1
(
\{\theta\}
is
the fractional part of
\theta
) and in the case
\{\theta\}+\beta>1
this
extension belongs to the space
C^{\theta+\beta^{\prime}}
with an arbitrary

\beta^{\prime}<1-\{\theta\}
. Because of this the following estimate is
valid

|\delta_{-\tau,t}^{m}f(t)|\leq\genfrac{}{.}{0.0pt}{0}{C(f)\tau^{\theta+\gamma},\quad t<1}{C(f),\quad t>1,},\quad\gamma=\min\{\beta,\beta^{\prime}\},

(6.45)

where we preserve the same notation
f(t)
for the extension.

Suppose first that the function
f(t)
is more smooth in the sense that it
possess the continuous derivative of order
n=[\theta]+1
(
n-1<\theta<n
).
Besides, we first suppose that
f(t)
is integrable and even finite (for
simplicity) for
t\rightarrow+\infty
. Then the Fourier transform of the
derivative
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f(t)
from (
1.9
) is calculated in, for
example,
[
1
]
, section 7 and it is equal to (see Lemma
8.4

below and Remark
8.5
after it)

F(D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f)(\xi)\equiv\widehat{D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f}(\xi)=(i\xi)^{[\theta]}(i\xi)^{\{\theta\}}\widehat{f}(\xi).

(6.46)

Here
\widehat{f}(\xi)
the Fourier transform of
f(t)
and
(i\xi)^{\{\theta\}}
is the following analytic extension of the function

z^{\{\theta\}}
from the positive real axis to the right half-plane

\operatorname{Re}z>0

(i\xi)^{\{\theta\}}=|\xi|^{\{\theta\}}e^{i\theta\frac{\pi}{2}sign\xi}

(6.47)

Calculate now the Fourier image of the integral
T_{\theta,m}(f)(t)
in the
right hand side of (
6.44
). We have (comp.
[
1
]
, section 25.4,
Lemma 25.3)

\widehat{T_{\theta,m}(f)}(\xi)={\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}}\frac{\widehat{\delta_{-\tau,t}^{m}f(t)}}{\tau^{1+\theta}}d\tau=\widehat{f}(\xi){\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}}\frac{(1-e^{-i\tau\xi})^{m}}{\tau^{1+[\theta]+\{\theta\}}}d\tau.

(6.48)

Make in the last integral the change of variables
z=i\tau\xi
. After such
change the last integral for
\xi>0
is transformed into the integral along
positive imaginary axis in the positive direction that is

\widehat{T_{\theta,m}(f)}(\xi)=(i\xi)^{[\theta]}(i\xi)^{\{\theta\}}\widehat{f}(\xi){\int\limits_{0i}^{\infty i}}\frac{(1-e^{-z})^{m}}{z^{1+[\theta]+\{\theta\}}}dz.

(6.49)

Consider for
R>0
the integral of the last integrand along the circular
contour

C_{R}^{+}=[0i,Ri]+[R\cdot e^{i\varphi},\varphi\in(\pi/2,0)]-[0,R]

This contour consists of the interval of the imaginary axis
[0i,Ri]
, of the
clock-wise oriented quarter of circle
[R\cdot e^{i\varphi},\varphi\in(\pi/2,0)]
, and of the negatively oriented interval
[0,R]
of the real axis.
Since the integrand is analytic and continuous in the right half-plane, the
integral along this contour
C_{R}^{+}
is equal to zero. Besides,

\left|{\int\limits_{[R\cdot e^{i\varphi},\varphi\in(\pi/2,0)]}}\frac{(1-e^{-z})^{m}}{z^{1+[\theta]+\{\theta\}}}dz\right|\leq\frac{2^{m}}{R^{1+[\theta]+\{\theta\}}}\frac{\pi}{2}R\rightarrow 0,\quad R\rightarrow\infty.

Consequently, letting
R\rightarrow\infty
, we obtain un view of the
directions of integration

{\int\limits_{0i}^{\infty i}}\frac{(1-e^{-z})^{m}}{z^{1+[\theta]+\{\theta\}}}dz={\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}}\frac{(1-e^{-x})^{m}}{x^{1+\theta}}dx\equiv 1/C(m,\theta)>0.

If now
\xi<0
, then the last integral in (
6.48
) after the change

z=i\tau\xi
goes to an analogous to (
6.49
) integral, but along the
negatively oriented imaginary axis. The analogous considerations for this case
give the same result that is

{\int\limits_{0i}^{-\infty i}}\frac{(1-e^{-z})^{m}}{z^{1+[\theta]+\{\theta\}}}dz={\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}}\frac{(1-e^{-x})^{m}}{x^{1+\theta}}dx\equiv 1/C(m,\theta)>0

with the same constant
C(m,\theta)
. Thus for any sign of
\xi
we get

\widehat{T_{\theta,m}(f)}(\xi)=C(m,\theta)(i\xi)^{[\theta]}(i\xi)^{\{\theta\}}\widehat{f}(\xi).

(6.50)

From (
6.46
) and (
6.50
) it follows that
\widehat{D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f}(\xi)=C(m,\theta)\widehat{T_{\theta,m}(f)}(\xi)
,
\xi\in(-\infty,\infty)
, which proves assertion (
6.44
) of the lemma for finite
functions
f(t)
of the class
C^{[\theta]+1}([0,\infty))
.

General case is obtained now by a mollifying and by a cutting off the function

f(t)
with a subsequent limiting process in the final equality (
6.44
),
which does not depend on extra smoothness.

On the ground of Lemma
6.10
, by replication of the proof of Proposition

6.3
, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 6.11

For a function
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with

u_{t}^{(k)}(x,0)=0,\quad k=0,...,[\theta+\theta\alpha]

the following estimate is valid

|D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\leq C(N,\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)|u|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{\left(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha\right)}.

(6.51)

Moreover, for the Hölder seminorm of the derivative
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u

with respect to a group
z_{k}
of the space variables we have the following
interpolation inequality with an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0

\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\theta\alpha)}\leq C\varepsilon^{\alpha}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\theta+\theta\alpha)}+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha)}.

(6.52)

Presenting this Proposition without a proof, we nevertheless note the
following. First of all, to make use of Lemma
6.10
and representation
(
6.44
), we need to extend the function
u(x,t)
to a finite in
t

function from the domain
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
to the domain
t>T
that is to
whole subspace
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}=R^{N}\times[0,\infty)
. This
can be done with a controlled preservation of the norm in
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
in the way,
which is described in, for example,
[
35
]
,
[
37
]
. In the
second place, extended further by zero in the domain
t<0
function
u(x,t)

preserves it’s norm in
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(R^{N}\times R^{1})
. It is precisely this place, where we need all the
derivatives of
u(x,t)
in
t
up to the order
[\theta+\theta\alpha]

(possibly bigger than
[\theta]
as it is required in Lemma
6.10
) to
vanish at
t=0
. After this the proof of the above Proposition coincides with
the proof of Proposition
6.1
with the making use of the representation
from Lemma
6.10
for
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u
(for obtaining the smoothness
in the space variables) and the proof of Proposition
3.1
(the
smoothness in
t
). The only difference is that the interval of integration

(0,\infty)
in representation (
6.44
) for
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u
is
naturally split not into a centered at zero ball and it’s outer part (as in
the proof of Proposition
6.1
), but into the intervals
(0,\varepsilon|h|^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{\theta}})
and
(\varepsilon|h|^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{\theta}},\infty)
.

7
Some additional technical assertions on the properties of fractional
differentiation in anisotropic Hölder spaces.

In the present section we prove several useful for applications assertions of
the interpolation type on the properties of fractional differentiation in
anisotropic Hölder spaces.

Lemma 7.1

Let a function
u(x,t)
is defined in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
and
possesses bounded in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
fractional derivative
D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u(x,t)
of a noninteger order
\mu>0
. Let also this derivative
possesses the smoothness of a noninteger order
\beta>0
with respect to a
group
z_{k}\in R^{N_{k}}
of the space variables that is

\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}=\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{R_{T}^{N}},\overline{h}\in R^{N_{k}},\overline{h}\neq 0}\frac{|\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u(x,t)|}{|\overline{h}|^{\beta}}<\infty,\quad m>\beta.

(7.1)

Here
\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)
is the finite difference of order

m
from the function
D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u(x,t)
in the variables
z_{k}
with
the step
\overline{h}\in R^{N_{k}}
.

Then

\left|\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)\right|\leq C\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}\tau^{\mu}|\overline{h}|^{\beta},\quad p\geq[\mu]+1,m>\beta,

(7.2)

where
\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}v(x,t)
is the finite difference in
t
of step

\tau>0
from the function
v(x,t)
.

If a function
u(x,t)
possesses bounded in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
derivative

u_{t}^{(n)}(x,t)
in
t
of an integer order
n>0
, which possesses the
smoothness in a space group
z_{k}\in R^{N_{k}}
of a noninteger order

\beta>0
that is

\left\langle u_{t}^{(n)}\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}=\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{R_{T}^{N}},\overline{h}\in R^{N_{k}},\overline{h}\neq 0}\frac{|\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u_{t}^{(n)}(x,t)|}{|\overline{h}|^{\beta}}<\infty,\quad m>\beta,

(7.3)

then also

\left|\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)\right|\leq C\left\langle u_{t}^{(n)}\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}\tau^{n}|\overline{h}|^{\beta},\quad m>\beta,\,p\geq n.

(7.4)

Proof.
First of all, the case of an integer order
n>0
follows directly from the
mean value theorem and from condition (
7.3
) - see formulas
(
7.14
), (
7.15
) below. Therefore we consider only the case of a
noninteger derivative.

Further, we can assume without loss of generality that the function
u(x,t)

possesses the property

\frac{\partial^{k}u(x,0)}{\partial t^{k}}\equiv 0,\quad k=0,...,[\mu].

(7.5)

In the opposite case we can replace
u(x,t)
with the function

\overline{u}(x,t)\equiv u(x,t)-{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{[\mu]}}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\frac{\partial^{k}u(x,0)}{\partial t^{k}}

since
\delta_{\tau,t}^{[\mu]+1}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)=\delta_{\tau,t}^{[\mu]+1}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}\overline{u}(x,t)
and

D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u=D_{\ast t}^{\mu}\overline{u}
. But for a function
u(x,t)

with (
7.5
) the following representation is valid (see
[
3
]
,
section 3.5)

D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u(x,t)=D_{\ast t}^{\{\mu\}}\left[u_{t}^{([\mu])}(x,t)\right],

(7.6)

u(x,t)=J_{\{\mu\}}\left\{D_{\ast t}^{\{\mu\}}\left[u_{t}^{([\mu])}(x,t)\right]\right\}.

Here
J_{\mu}
is the operator of fractional integration of order
\mu
that
is

u(x,t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\mu)}{\int\limits_{0}^{t}}(t-\tau)^{-1+\mu}D_{\ast\tau}^{\mu}u(x,\tau)d\tau=

(7.7)

=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\{\mu\})}{\int\limits_{0}^{t}}(t-\tau)^{-1+\{\mu\}}D_{\ast\tau}^{\mu}u_{\tau}^{([\mu])}(x,\tau)d\tau.

Note also that we can assume
p=[\mu]+1
.

Let first
\mu\in(0,1)
that is
\mu=\{\mu\}
,
[\mu]=0
. Then with
m>\beta

\delta_{\tau,t}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\mu)}\delta_{\tau,t}\left\{{\int\limits_{0}^{t}}(t-\omega)^{-1+\mu}\left[\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}D_{\ast\omega}^{\mu}u(x,\omega)\right]d\omega\right\}.

(7.8)

Denote

v(x,t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\mu)}\left[\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}D_{\ast\tau}^{\mu}u(x,t)\right],

(7.9)

and remark that according to (
7.1
),

\left|v(x,t)\right|\leq C(\mu)\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}|\overline{h}|^{\beta}.

(7.10)

Assuming that
t>2\tau
, represent the difference
\delta_{\tau,t}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)
in the form

\delta_{\tau,t}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)={\int\limits_{t-2\tau}^{t+\tau}}(t+\tau-\omega)^{-1+\mu}v(x,\omega)d\omega-

-{\int\limits_{t-2\tau}^{t}}(t-\omega)^{-1+\mu}v(x,\omega)d\omega+

+{\int\limits_{0}^{t-2\tau}}\left[(t+\tau-\omega)^{-1+\mu}-(t-\omega)^{-1+\mu}\right]v(x,\omega)d\omega\equiv I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

(7.11)

Taking into account (
7.10
) we have for
I_{1}

|I_{1}|\leq C(\mu)\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}|\overline{h}|^{\beta}{\int\limits_{t-2\tau}^{t+\tau}}(t+\tau-\omega)^{-1+\mu}d\omega=

=C(\mu)\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}|\overline{h}|^{\beta}\tau^{\mu}.

The integral
I_{2}
is estimated analogously, which gives

|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|\leq C(\mu)\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}|\overline{h}|^{\beta}\tau^{\mu}.

(7.12)

To estimate
I_{3}
we make use of the mean value theorem

I_{3}=C(\mu)\tau{\int\limits_{0}^{t-2\tau}}(t+\varkappa(t-\omega)\tau-\omega)^{-2+\mu}v(x,\omega)d\omega,\quad\varkappa(t-\omega)\in(0,1),

and note that since
\omega<t-2\tau
and consequently
\tau<(t-\omega)/2
,
then

|t-\omega|\leq|t+\varkappa(t-\omega)\tau-\omega|\leq\frac{3}{2}|t-\omega|.

Therefore

|I_{2}|\leq C\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}|\overline{h}|^{\beta}\tau{\int\limits_{0}^{t-2\tau}}(t-\omega)^{-2+\mu}d\omega\leq

\leq C\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}|\overline{h}|^{\beta}\tau{\int\limits_{-\infty}^{t-2\tau}}(t-\omega)^{-2+\mu}d\omega=C\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}|\overline{h}|^{\beta}\tau^{\mu}.

(7.13)

If now
t<2\tau
, then we can represent the difference in the form

\delta_{\tau,t}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)=\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t+\tau)-\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)
, estimate
modulo each term of the difference separately analogously to the estimates of
the integrals
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
in (
7.12
), and, taking into account
(
7.12
), (
7.13
), arrive at estimate (
7.2
) in the case

\mu\in(0,1)
.

Let now
\mu>1
. We make use of the integral mean value theorem to represent
the difference in
t
of order
[\mu]
as follows

\delta_{\tau,t}^{[\mu]}u(x,t)=\tau^{[\mu]}{\int\limits_{0}^{1}}d\xi_{1}...{\int\limits_{0}^{1}}d\xi_{[\mu]}u_{t}^{[\mu]}(x,t+\xi_{1}\tau+...\xi_{1}\tau)=

=\tau^{[\mu]}{\int\limits_{P_{[\mu]}}}u_{t}^{[\mu]}\left(x,t+\tau{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{[\mu]}}\xi_{i}\right)d\xi,

(7.14)

where
\xi=(\xi_{1},...,\xi_{[\mu]})\in P_{[\mu]}=\{\xi:0<\xi_{i}<1\}
.
Then the double difference
\delta_{\tau,t}^{[\mu]+1}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)
is equal to

\delta_{\tau,t}^{[\mu]+1}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)=\delta_{\tau,t}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)=\tau^{[\mu]}{\int\limits_{P_{[\mu]}}}\delta_{\tau,t}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u_{t}^{[\mu]}\left(x,t+\tau{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{[\mu]}}\xi_{i}\right)d\xi.

(7.15)

At the same time, according to the conditions of the lemma, the function

u(x,t)
possesses the fractional derivative
D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u(x,t)
, which
satisfies (
7.1
). Therefore at a fixed
\tau
and
\xi
the function

v(x,t)=u_{t}^{[\mu]}\left(x,t+\tau{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{[\mu]}}\xi_{i}\right)
admits, by virtue of (
7.6
), the fractional derivative

D_{\ast t}^{\{\mu\}}v(x,t)
with (
7.1
). Therefore by what was proved
above

\left|\delta_{\tau,t}^{[\mu]+1}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)\right|\leq\tau^{[\mu]}{\int\limits_{P_{[\mu]}}}\left|\delta_{\tau,t}\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u_{t}^{[\mu]}\left(x,t+\tau{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{[\mu]}}\xi_{i}\right)\right|d\xi\leq

\leq C\tau^{[\mu]}\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}|\overline{h}|^{\beta}\tau^{\{\mu\}}=C\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\mu}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}|\overline{h}|^{\beta}\tau^{\mu},

which finishes the proof of the lemma.

Theorem 7.2

Let a function
u(x,t)
is defined in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
and
possesses bounded in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
derivative
D_{t}^{\theta}u(x,t)

of an integer or a noninteger order
\theta>0
and in the case of a noninteger
order we mean the Caputo - Jrbashyan derivative
D_{*t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
.
Suppose that the derivative
D_{t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
possesses the smoothness of
a noninteger order
\beta>0
with respect to a spacial group
z_{k}\in R^{N_{k}}
that is

\left\langle D_{t}^{\theta}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}=\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{R_{T}^{N}},\overline{h}\in R^{N_{k}},\overline{h}\neq 0}\frac{|\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)|}{|\overline{h}|^{\beta}}<\infty,\quad m>\beta.

(7.16)

Besides, suppose that the function
u(x,t)
itself possesses the smoothness of
a noninteger order
\gamma>\max\{1,\beta\}
with respect to the same variables

z_{k}\in R^{N_{k}}
that is

\left\langle u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\gamma)}=\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{R_{T}^{N}},\overline{h}\in R^{N_{k}},\overline{h}\neq 0}\frac{|\delta_{\overline{h},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)|}{|\overline{h}|^{\gamma}}<\infty,\quad m>\gamma.

(7.17)

Then a derivative of
u(x,t)
of an integer order
\beta<n<\gamma
with
respect to
z_{k}\in R^{N_{k}}

D_{z_{k}}^{\overline{\rho}}u(x,t)=\frac{\partial^{\rho_{1}}...\partial^{\rho_{N_{k}}}u(x,t)}{\partial x_{i_{1}}^{\rho_{1}}...\partial x_{i_{N_{k}}}^{\rho_{N_{k}}}},\,\overline{\rho}=(\rho_{1},...,\rho_{N_{k}}),\,\rho=\rho_{1}+...+\rho_{N_{k}}=n,

possesses the smoothness with respect to
t
of order
\theta(\gamma-n)/(\gamma-\beta)
that is

\left\langle D_{z_{k}}^{\overline{\rho}}u\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\theta(\gamma-n)/(\gamma-\beta))}=\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{R_{T}^{N}},\tau>0}\frac{|\delta_{\tau,t}^{m}D_{z_{k}}^{\overline{\rho}}u(x,t)|}{\tau^{\theta(\gamma-n)/(\gamma-\beta)}}<\infty,\quad m>\theta(\gamma-n)/(\gamma-\beta).

Moreover,

\left\langle D_{z_{k}}^{\overline{\rho}}u\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\theta(\gamma-n)/(\gamma-\beta))}\leq C(\theta,\beta,\gamma,n)\left(\varepsilon\left\langle u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\gamma)}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{c}}\left\langle D_{t}^{\theta}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}\right),

(7.18)

where
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary.

Note that in the case
n<\beta
the derivative
D_{z_{k}}^{\overline{\rho}}u(x,t)
just admits the derivative
D_{t}^{\theta}D_{z_{k}}^{\overline{\rho}}u(x,t)
in
t
as it follows from the first condition of the theorem.

Proof.
Let first
\beta\in(0,1)
. We prove now the assertion of the theorem for
n=1

that is for the derivative of the first order with respect to a single spacial
variable
x_{l}
from the group
z_{k}
. For that we use the schema of
reasonings from
[
32
]
, section 3, Lemma 2, when obtaining formula
(
2.6
) there. Let for the simplicity of notations
l=1
that is we
consider the derivative
u_{x_{1}}(x,t)
. Consider the following
representation for the finite difference from
u_{x_{1}}(x,t)
of order

m>\gamma
with a step
\xi>0
with respect to
x_{1}

\delta_{\xi,x_{1}}^{m}u_{x_{1}}(x,t)={\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m}}(-1)^{m-j}C_{m}^{j}u_{x_{1}}(x_{1}+j\xi,x_{2},...x_{N},t).

Expressing from this the term without a shift
u_{x_{1}}(x,t)
over the others
terms of the identity and integrating over
\xi
in the range from zero to

\varepsilon\tau^{a}
,
\tau>0
,
\varepsilon>0
,
a=\theta/(\gamma-\beta)
,
we obtain

u_{x_{1}}(x,t)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}}(-1)^{j+1}\frac{C_{m}^{j}}{j}{\int\limits_{0}^{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}}u_{\xi}(x_{1}+j\xi,x_{2},...x_{N},t)d\xi+

+\frac{(-1)^{m}}{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}{\int\limits_{0}^{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}}\delta_{\xi,x_{1}}^{m}u_{x_{1}}(x,t)d\xi=

={\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}B_{m,j}\left[u(x_{1}+j\varepsilon\tau^{a},x_{2},...x_{N},t)-u(x_{1},x_{2},...x_{N},t)\right]+

+\frac{(-1)^{m}}{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}{\int\limits_{0}^{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}}\delta_{\xi,x_{1}}^{m}u_{x_{1}}(x,t)d\xi\equiv{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}}I_{j}+I_{0}.

(7.19)

To estimate the smoothness of
u_{x_{1}}(x,t)
in
t
we estimate a finite
difference along this variable of a sufficiently high order
\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}u_{x_{1}}(x,t)
,
p>\theta
. And for this we estimate the finite
differences of the terms
I_{j}
and
I_{0}
in (
7.19
). For the terms

I_{j}
we have

\left|\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}I_{j}\right|=\frac{C}{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}\left|\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}\delta_{j\varepsilon\tau^{a},x_{1}}u(x,t)\right|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}\left\langle D_{t}^{\theta}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}\tau^{\theta}(\varepsilon\tau^{a})^{\beta},

where we made use of Lemma
7.1
. Thus, since
a=\theta/(\gamma-\beta)
,

\left|\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}I_{j}\right|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1-\beta}}\left\langle D_{t}^{\theta}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}\tau^{\theta(\gamma-1)/(\gamma-\beta)}.

(7.20)

Further, since for each fixed
t>0
the function
u_{x_{1}}(x,t)
has the
order of smoothness
\gamma-1
in
x_{1}
, then

|I_{0}|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}\left\langle u_{x_{1}}\right\rangle_{x_{1},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\gamma-1)}{\int\limits_{0}^{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}}\xi^{\gamma-1}d\xi\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon\tau^{a}}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\gamma)}(\varepsilon\tau^{a})^{\gamma}=

=C\varepsilon^{\gamma-1}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\gamma)}\tau^{\theta(\gamma-1)/(\gamma-\beta)}.

Consequently,

\left|\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}I_{0}\right|\leq C(p)\varepsilon^{\gamma-1}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\gamma)}\tau^{\theta(\gamma-1)/(\gamma-\beta)}.

(7.21)

The assertion of the theorem for
n=1
under the condition
\beta\in(0,1)

follows now from (
7.20
) and (
7.21
) with taking into account
(
7.19
). Thus, the derivatives of the first order in
x_{i}
from
z_{k}

belongs, under fixed spacial variables from other groups, to the space

C_{z_{k},\,\hskip 8.19447ptt}^{\gamma-1,\theta(\gamma-1)/(\gamma-\beta)}(R_{T}^{N_{k}})
and estimate (
7.18
) is valid for
n=1
. But since the derivatives in

z_{k}
of order
n>1
are the derivatives of order
n-1
of the first
derivatives, then estimate (
7.18
) for
n>1
follows now directly from
(
2.6
). Consequently, the theorem is proved for
\beta\in(0,1)
.

If
\beta>1
, then it is enough instead of the function
u(x,t)
itself to
consider it’s derivative
D_{z_{k}}^{\overline{\rho}}u(x,t)
of order

\rho=[\beta]
and denote this derivative by
v(x,t)=D_{z_{k}}^{\rho}u(x,t)
.
This function fully meets the conditions of the theorem with the same
\theta
, with
\{\beta\}\in(0,1)
instead of
\beta
, and with
\gamma-[\beta]

instead of
\gamma
. The application of what was proved above to this function

v(x,t)
with the subsequent recalculation of the exponents of smoothness
leads to the proof of the theorem in the general case.

The analogous assertion is valid and in the case, when instead of the usual
derivatives in a spacial group
z_{k}
one considers the fractional Laplace
operator with respect to
z_{k}
.

Theorem 7.3

Let a function
u(x,t)
be defined in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
and
let it’s derivative
D_{t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
of order
\theta>0
be bounded in

\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
, where
\theta>0
can be either an integer or a
noninteger (in the case of a noninteger
\theta>0
we mean the Caputo -
Jrbashyan derivative). Suppose that the derivative
D_{t}^{\theta}u(x,t)

possesses the smoothness in
z_{k}\in R^{N_{k}}
of a noninteger order

\beta>0
in the sense of (
7.16
). Suppose also that the function

u(x,t)
itself possesses the smoothness in
z_{k}\in R^{N_{k}}
of a
noninteger order
\gamma>\beta
in the sense of (
7.17
). Then the
fractional Laplace operator of this function
(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\mu}{2}}u(x,t)
in
z_{k}\in R^{N_{k}}
of order
\mu\in(\beta,\gamma)
possesses
the smoothness in
t
of order
\theta(\gamma-\mu)/(\gamma-\beta)
that is

\left\langle(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{{}^{\frac{\mu}{2}}}u\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\theta(\gamma-\mu)/(\gamma-\beta))}=\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{R_{T}^{N}},\tau>0}\frac{|\delta_{\tau,t}^{m}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{{}^{\frac{\mu}{2}}}u(x,t)|}{\tau^{\theta(\gamma-\mu)/(\gamma-\beta)}}<\infty,m>\frac{\theta(\gamma-\mu)}{(\gamma-\beta)}.

Moreover,

\left\langle(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{{}^{\frac{\mu}{2}}}u\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\theta(\gamma-\mu)/(\gamma-\beta))}\leq

\leq C(\theta,\beta,\gamma,\mu)\left(\varepsilon^{\gamma-\mu}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\gamma)}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\left(\mu-\beta\right)}}\left\langle D_{t}^{\theta}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}\right),

(7.22)

where
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary.

Note that for
\mu<\beta
the Laplace operator
(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{{}^{\frac{\mu}{2}}}u(x,t)
just has the derivative
D_{t}^{\theta}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\mu}u(x,t)
, as it follows from the first assumption of the theorem.

Proof.
We use representation (
1.12
)

(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{{}^{\frac{\mu}{2}}}u(x,t)=C_{N_{k},\mu,m}{\int\limits_{\eta_{k}\in R^{N_{k}}}}\frac{\delta_{\eta_{k},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)}{|\eta_{k}|^{N_{k}+\mu}}d\eta_{k},

(7.23)

where we choose
m>\gamma
. Consider, as in the previous theorem, the
difference in
t
of an order
p>\theta
with a step
\tau

D(\tau)\equiv\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{{}^{\frac{\mu}{2}}}u(x,t)=C_{N_{k},\mu,m}{\int\limits_{\eta_{k}\in R^{N_{k}}}}\frac{\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}\delta_{\eta_{k},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)}{|\eta_{k}|^{N_{k}+\mu}}d\eta_{k}.

(7.24)

Split the integral in (
7.24
) into the two ones

D(\tau)=C_{N_{k},\mu,m}{\int\limits_{\left|\eta_{k}\right|\leq\varepsilon\tau^{a}}}\frac{\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}\delta_{\eta_{k},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)}{|\eta_{k}|^{N_{k}+\mu}}d\eta_{k}+

(7.25)

+C_{N_{k},\mu,m}\int\limits_{\left|\eta_{k}\right|>\varepsilon\tau^{a}}\frac{\delta_{\tau,t}^{p}\delta_{\eta_{k},z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)}{|\eta_{k}|^{N_{k}+\mu}}d\eta_{k}\equiv I_{1}+I_{2},

where
\varepsilon>0
,
a=\theta/(\gamma-\beta)
. Bearing in mind
(
7.17
), we can obtain for
I_{1}
the estimate

\left|I_{1}\right|\leq C_{N_{k},\mu,m}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\gamma)}{\int\limits_{\left|\eta_{k}\right|\leq\varepsilon\tau^{a}}}\frac{|\eta_{k}|^{\gamma}}{|\eta_{k}|^{N_{k}+\mu}}d\eta_{k}=

=C_{N_{k},\mu,m}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\gamma)}\left(\varepsilon\tau^{a}\right)^{\gamma-\mu}=C_{N_{k},\mu,m}\varepsilon^{\gamma-\mu}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\gamma)}\tau^{\theta(\gamma-\mu)/(\gamma-\beta)}.

(7.26)

To estimate
I_{2}
we make use of (
7.16
) together with Lemma

7.1
, which gives

\left|I_{2}\right|\leq C_{N_{k},\mu,m}\left\langle D_{t}^{\theta}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}\tau^{\theta}{\int\limits_{\left|\eta_{k}\right|>\varepsilon\tau^{a}}}\frac{|\eta_{k}|^{\beta}}{|\eta_{k}|^{N_{k}+\mu}}d\eta_{k}=

=C_{N_{k},\mu,m}\left\langle D_{t}^{\theta}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}\tau^{\theta}\left(\varepsilon\tau^{a}\right)^{-\left(\mu-\beta\right)}=

=C_{N_{k},\mu,m}\left\langle D_{t}^{\theta}u\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\beta)}\varepsilon^{-\left(\mu-\beta\right)}\tau^{\theta(\gamma-\mu)/(\gamma-\beta)}.

The assertion of the theorem follows now from (
7.24
) - (
7.26
) in
view of (
2.4
).

8
Theorems on Fourier multipliers in Hölder spaces

In this section we present some theorems from
[
39
]
and
[
40
]
on
the Fourier multipliers in Hölder spaces. We consider the multipliers,
that act either in spaces with finite Hölder seminorm with respect to all
independent variables or in spaces with finite Hölder seminorm with
respect to a part of independent variables. These theorems will be applied
further for the proofs of theorems
2.1
-
2.5
.

We follow
[
40
]
to give some necessary definitions.

Let
K>0
be an integer and let

\gamma\in(0,1),\quad\beta=(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{K}),\quad\beta_{1}=1,\quad\beta_{i}\in(0,1],i=2,...,K.

(8.1)

Consider the Hölder space
C^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})
with the norm

\left\|u\right\|_{C^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})}\equiv\left|u\right|_{R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta)}\equiv|u|_{R^{K}}^{(0)}+{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{x_{i},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta_{i})}.

(8.2)

Along with this space we consider the more narrow space
H^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})
with the norm

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})}\equiv\left\|u\right\|_{L_{2}(R^{K})}+{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{x_{i},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta_{i})},

(8.3)

and it was shown in
[
39
]
that

\left|u\right|_{R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta)}\leq C(\gamma,\beta)\left\|u\right\|_{H^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})}.

(8.4)

Let a measurable and bounded function
\widetilde{m}(\xi)
,
\xi\in R^{K}
, be
defined in
R^{K}
. Define the operator
M:H^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})\rightarrow L_{2}(R^{K})
as follows

Mu\equiv F^{-1}\left[\widetilde{m}(\xi)F(u)(\xi)\right]\equiv F^{-1}\left[\widetilde{m}(\xi)\widetilde{u}(\xi)\right].

(8.5)

Here
F(u)(\xi)\equiv\widetilde{u}(\xi)
is the Fourier transform of
u(x)

extended on the space
L_{2}(R^{K})
,
F^{-1}
is the inverse Fourier
transform. Since
u(x)\in H^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})\subset L_{2}(R^{K})
, and the
function
\widetilde{m}(\xi)
is bounded, the operator
M
is correctly
defined. We call the function
\widetilde{m}(\xi)
a Fourier multiplier.

Let the whole set of the variables
(\xi_{1},...,\xi_{K})=\xi
be split into

r
subsets of length
K_{i}
,
i=1,...,r
,
K=K_{1}+...+K_{r}
so that

\xi=(y_{1},...,y_{r}),\quad y_{1}=(\xi_{1},...,\xi_{K_{1}}),...,y_{r}=(\xi_{K_{1}+...K_{r-1}+1},...,\xi_{K}).

(8.6)

Let, further,
\omega_{i}
,
i=1,...,r
be multi-indexes each of length

K_{i}

\omega_{1}=(\omega_{1,1},...,\omega_{1,K_{1}}),...,\omega_{r}=(\omega_{r,1},...,\omega_{r,K_{r}}),\quad\omega_{i,j}\in\mathbf{N\cup\{0\}.}

(8.7)

Denote by
D_{y_{i}}^{\omega_{i}}\widetilde{u}(\xi)
the derivative of the
function
\widetilde{u}(\xi)
in the group
y_{i}
of order
|\omega_{i}|=\omega_{i,1}+...+\omega_{i,K_{i}}
that is
D_{y_{i}}^{\omega_{i}}\widetilde{u}(\xi)=D_{\xi_{j_{1}}}^{\omega_{i,1}}...D_{\xi_{j_{N_{i}}}}^{\omega_{i,N_{i}}}\widetilde{u}(\xi)
. Let also
p\in(1,2]
and positive
numbers
s_{i}
,
i=1,...,r
, satisfy the conditions

s_{i}>\frac{N_{i}}{p},\quad i=1,...,r.

(8.8)

Denote for
\nu>0

B_{\nu}=\{\xi\in R^{K}:\nu\leq|\xi|\leq\nu^{-1}\}.

(8.9)

Suppose that for some
\nu>0
the function
\widetilde{m}(\xi)
satisfies with
a certain
\mu>0
and uniformly in
\lambda>0
the condition

{\sum\limits_{|\omega_{i}|\leq s_{i}}}\left\|D_{y_{1}}^{\omega_{1}}D_{y_{2}}^{\omega_{2}}...D_{y_{r}}^{\omega_{r}}\widetilde{m}(\lambda^{\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}}\xi_{1},...,\lambda^{\frac{1}{\beta_{K}}}\xi_{K})\right\|_{L_{p}(B_{\nu})}\leq\mu,

(8.10)

where
\beta_{i}
are defined in (
8.1
).

Theorem 8.1

(
[
39
]
: T.2.1, L.2.1, L.2.2, T.2.2, T.2.3)

If a function
\widetilde{m}(\xi)
satisfies conditions (
8.10
), then the
defined in (
8.5
) operator
M
, is a bounded linear operator from the
space
H^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})
to itself and

\left\|Mu\right\|_{H^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})}\leq C(K,\gamma,\beta,p,\nu,\{s_{i}\})\mu\left\|u\right\|_{H^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})},

(8.11)

{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}}\left\langle Mu\right\rangle_{x_{i},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta_{i})}\leq C(K,\gamma,\beta,p,\nu,\{s_{i}\})\mu{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{x_{i},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta_{i})}.

(8.12)

Condition (
8.10
) can be especially easily verified in the cases, when
the function
\widetilde{m}(\xi)
has the homogeneity of degree zero that is
when
\widetilde{m}(\lambda^{\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}}\xi_{1},...,\lambda^{\frac{1}{\beta_{K}}}\xi_{K})=\widetilde{m}(\xi)
. Note also that condition
(
8.10
) includes the derivatives of
\widetilde{m}(\xi)
in
y_{i}
only
up to the orders
s_{i}
. The case
r=1
,
K_{1}=K
,
p=2
is considered in
Lemma 2.1 in
[
39
]
and Lemma 2.2 of the same paper contains the case

r=K
,
K_{i}=1
,
s_{i}=1
. The general case is analogous - see lemmas 2.2 -
2.4 in
[
40
]
.

Now we formulate a generalization of Theorem
8.1
to the case of Fourier
multipliers in the spaces of functions with the Hölder condition only with
respect to a part of the variables. For that we need to split the whole set of
variables
x\in R^{K}
and the corresponding dual (in the sense of the Fourier
transform) set of variables
\xi\in R^{K}
, besides splitting (
8.6
) and
regardless this splitting, also as follows.

Let
x=(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})
,
x^{(1)}=(x_{1},...,x_{S})\in R^{S}
,

x^{(2)}=(x_{S+1},...,x_{K})\in R^{K-S}
and correspondingly
\xi=(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})
,
\xi^{(1)}=(\xi_{1},...,\xi_{S})\in R^{S}
,
\xi^{(2)}=(\xi_{S+1},...,\xi_{K})\in R^{K-S}
. Let, further, analogously to
(
8.1
),

\gamma\in(0,1),\quad\beta=(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{S}),\quad\beta_{1}=1,\quad\beta_{i}\in(0,1],i=2,...,S,

\varkappa=(\varkappa_{S+1},...,\varkappa_{K}),\quad\varkappa_{i}>0,\quad i=S+1,...,K.

(8.13)

Note that
\varkappa_{i}
not necessarily belongs to
(0,1]
. Analogously to
(
8.3
) define the space
H_{x^{(1)},x^{(2)}}^{\gamma\beta,\hskip 8.19447pt\gamma\varkappa}(R^{K})=C_{x^{(1)},x^{(2)}}^{\gamma\beta,\hskip 8.19447pt\gamma\varkappa}(R^{K})\cap L_{2}(R^{K})
as the Banach space of functions with the finite
norm

\left\|u\right\|_{H_{x^{(1)},x^{(2)}}^{\gamma\beta,\hskip 7.3612pt\gamma\varkappa}(R^{K})}\equiv\left\|u\right\|_{L_{2}(R^{K})}+{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{S}}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{x_{i},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta_{i})}+{\sum\limits_{i=S+1}^{K}}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{x_{i},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\varkappa_{i})}

(8.14)

and analogously to (
8.4
)

\left|u\right|_{x^{(1)},x^{(2)},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta,\gamma\varkappa)}\leq C(\gamma,\beta)\left\|u\right\|_{H_{x^{(1)},x^{(2)}}^{\gamma\beta,\hskip 7.3612pt\gamma\varkappa}(R^{K})}.

(8.15)

Besides, define the Banach space
H_{x^{(1)}}^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})\supset C_{x^{(1)}}^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})\cap L_{2}(R^{K})
with the finite norm

\left\|u\right\|_{H_{x^{(1)}}^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})}\equiv\left\|u\right\|_{L_{2}(R^{K})}+{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{S}}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{x_{i},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta_{i})},

(8.16)

and we stress that the functions from
H_{x^{(1)}}^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})
have
bounded Hölder seminorms with respect to the variables from the group

x^{(1)}
only. In particular, the functions from this space are not
necessarily bounded - see an example before Theorem 2.7 in
[
40
]
.

Theorem 8.2

(
[
40
]
: T.2.7) Let a function

\widetilde{m}(\xi)\in C(R^{K}\backslash\{0\})\text{ be continuous and bounded
in }R^{K}\backslash\{0\}

(8.17)

and let it satisfy the condition

\widetilde{m}(\xi)|_{\xi^{(1)}=0}=\widetilde{m}(0,\xi^{(2)})\equiv 0,\quad\xi^{(2)}\in R^{K-S}\backslash\{0\}.

(8.18)

Let, further,
p\in(1,2]
and let positive numbers
s_{i}
,
i=1,...,r
,
satisfy the condition (comp. (
8.8
))

s_{i}>\frac{N_{i}}{p}+\gamma,\quad i=1,...,r.

(8.19)

Let, besides, the following condition be satisfied (comp. (
8.10
))

{\sum\limits_{|\omega_{i}|\leq s_{i}}}\left\|D_{y_{1}}^{\omega_{1}}D_{y_{2}}^{\omega_{2}}...D_{y_{r}}^{\omega_{r}}\widetilde{m}(\lambda^{\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}}\xi_{1},...,\lambda^{\frac{1}{\beta_{S}}}\xi_{S},\lambda^{\frac{1}{\varkappa_{S+1}}}\xi_{1},...,\lambda^{\frac{1}{\varkappa_{K}}}\xi_{S})\right\|_{L_{p}(B_{\nu})}\leq\mu,

(8.20)

where
B_{\nu}
is defined in (
8.9
) and
\mu
is a positive number.

Then the operator
M
from (
8.5
) is a bounded linear operator from the
space
H_{x^{(1)}}^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})
to the space
H_{x^{(1)},x^{(2)}}^{\gamma\beta,\hskip 8.19447pt\gamma\varkappa}(R^{K})
, and

\left\|Mu\right\|_{H_{x^{(1)},x^{(2)}}^{\gamma\beta,\hskip 7.3612pt\gamma\varkappa}(R^{K})}\leq C(K,\gamma,\beta,\varkappa,p,\nu,\{s_{i}\})\mu\left\|u\right\|_{H_{x^{(1)}}^{\gamma\beta}(R^{K})},

(4.22)

{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{S}}\left\langle Mu\right\rangle_{x_{i},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta_{i})}+{\sum\limits_{i=S+1}^{K}}\left\langle Mu\right\rangle_{x_{i},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\varkappa_{i})}\leq C(K,\gamma,\beta,\varkappa,p,\nu,\{s_{i}\})\mu{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{S}}\left\langle u\right\rangle_{x_{i},R^{K}}^{(\gamma\beta_{i})}.

(4.23)

As the conclusion of the section we present two auxiliary statements we need
in what follows.

Lemma 8.3

(
[
40
]
: L.2.8) Let a function
\widetilde{f}(i\xi_{0},\xi)

(
i
is the imaginary unit,
\xi_{0}\in R^{1}
,
\xi\in R^{N}
) be defined in

R^{N+1}
and can be extended to a function
\widetilde{f}(i\xi_{0}+a,\xi)
in
the domain
a\geq 0
in the way that the extension
\widetilde{f}(i\xi_{0}+a,\xi)
possesses the properties:

1)
\widetilde{f}(i\xi_{0}+a,\xi)
is continuous in the domain
a\geq 0
;

2) the function
\widetilde{f}(i\xi_{0}+a,\xi)
is analytic in the domain

a>0
with respect to the variable
p=i\xi_{0}+a
;

3) this function satisfies in the domain
a\geq 0
the following inequality
with some constants
C>0
,
m_{1}
, and
m_{2}

\left|\widetilde{f}(p,\xi)\right|\leq C(1+|p|)^{m_{1}}(1+|\xi|)^{m_{2}}.

(8.21)

Then the inverse Fourier transform of this function ( including in the sense
of distributions )

f(t,x)=(2\pi)^{-(N+1)}{\int\limits_{R^{N+1}}}e^{it\xi_{0}+ix\xi}\widetilde{f}(i\xi_{0},\xi)d\xi d\xi_{0}

vanishes for
t<0

f(t,x)\equiv 0,\quad t<0.

(8.22)

Lemma 8.4

Let
f(t)\in C^{n}([0,\infty))
,
n\geq 1
,
f^{(k)}(t)\in L_{1}([0,\infty))
,
k=0,1,...,n
and
f^{(k)}(0)=0
,
k=0,1,...,n-1
and let

\theta\in(n-1,n)
. Then the fractional derivative
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f(t)
,
extended along with the function
f(t)
itself, in the domain
\{t<0\}
by
zero, has the following Fourier transform

\widehat{D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f(t)}(\xi_{0})\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}}D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f(t)e^{-i\xi_{0}t}dt,

(8.23)

where the integral is understood as an improper one. Moreover,

\widehat{D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f(t)}(\xi_{0})=(i\xi_{0})^{[\theta]}(i\xi_{0})^{\{\theta\}}\widehat{f}(\xi_{0}),

(8.24)

where
\widehat{f}(\xi_{0})
is the Fourier transform of
f(t)
and

(i\xi_{0})^{\{\theta\}}\equiv|\xi_{0}|^{\{\theta\}}e^{i\{\theta\}\frac{\pi}{2}sign(\xi_{0})}

(8.25)

is an analytic extension of the function
z^{\{\theta\}}
from the positive
real half-axis to the right half-plane
\operatorname{Re}z>0
.

Remark 8.5

We do not present here a detailed proof of this statement because
it, by essence, is contained in, for example,
[
1
]
, Section 7. We note
only that at applications of
[
1
]
, Section 7, one should take into
account that the monograph
[
1
]
considers the direct Fourier transform
with
e^{ix\xi_{0}}
and the present paper makes use in (
1.10
) of the
kernel
e^{-ix\xi_{0}}
. Besides, it should be taken into account that, since

f(t)\equiv 0
for
t<0
, then

D_{\ast t}^{\theta}f(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\theta)}{\int\limits_{0}^{t}}\frac{f^{(n)}(\tau)d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{\theta-n+1}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\theta)}{\int\limits_{-\infty}^{t}}\frac{f^{(n)}(\tau)d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{\theta-n+1}}.

9
Cauchy problem for equation (
1.2
) in the case of a noninteger

\theta\in(0,1)
,
\theta\alpha\in(0,1)
.

In the present section we study problem (
1.2
), (
1.3
) with

\theta\in(0,1)
in the anisotropic Hölder spaces
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
from (
2.8
). In
this way, consider the following Cauchy problem for a defined in

\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
unknown function
u(x,t)

Lu(x,t)\equiv D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u(x,t)=f(x,t),\quad(x,t)\in R_{T}^{N},

(9.1)

u(x,0)=u_{0}(x),\quad x\in R^{N},

(9.2)

where

\theta\in(0,1),\quad\sigma_{k}>0,\quad k=1,...,r,

(9.3)

and the given functions belong to the spaces

f(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}}),\quad u_{0}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)}(R^{N}).

(9.4)

In this section we suppose that
\alpha
is so small that the following
condition is satisfied

\theta,\theta\alpha\in(0,1),\quad\sigma_{k}\alpha\in(0,1),\quad k=1,...,r.

(9.5)

Besides, the following compatibility conditions are expected to be met ( see
condition (
2.16
) and Remark
2.4
)

f(x,0)={\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u_{0}(x),\quad x\in R^{N}.

(9.6)

The following theorem is valid.

Theorem 9.1

If conditions (
9.3
) - (
9.6
) are satisfied, then
problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) has the unique solution
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})

with the following estimates

|u|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha,T)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

(9.7)

\left\langle u\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

(9.8)

\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right)\widetilde{T}^{\theta+\theta\alpha}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(0)},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq T.

(9.9)

The subsequent content of the section consists of the proof of the theorem and
this proof will be presented in several steps.

9.1
Reduction of the initial data.

Note first that we can confine ourselves to the case

u_{0}(x)\equiv 0,\quad f(x,0)\equiv 0,\quad x\in R^{N}.

(9.10)

Indeed, making in problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) the change of the unknown
function

u(x,t)\rightarrow v(x,t)=u(x,t)-u_{0}(x),

we see that
v(x,t)
satisfies zero initial condition (
9.2
) and the
equation

D_{\ast t}^{\theta}v+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}v=\widetilde{f(}x,t)\equiv f(x,t)-{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u_{0}(x).

From Proposition
6.3
it follows that
\widetilde{f(}x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
, as well as
the original function
f(x,t)
, and besides

|\widetilde{f}|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(9.11)

Moreover, by virtue of compatibility condition (
9.6
), the condition

\widetilde{f(}x,0)\equiv 0
is satisfied. Thus, we will assume below that
conditions (
9.10
) are satisfied.

9.2
Extension of the data.

The function
f(x,t)
can be extended in the domain
t>T
to a finite in
t

function with the preserving of the class
C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha}
and with the qualified preserving of the norm

|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}.

(9.12)

The way of such extension is described in, for example,
[
35
]
,

[
37
]
, Ch.4 (in our case under condition (
9.5
) the function

f(x,t)
can be simply extended in the even way trough the point
t=T
with a
subsequent cut-off with respect to
t
).

Besides, since the second condition in (
9.10
) is satisfied and by virtue
of the first condition in (
9.5
), we can assume that
f(x,t)
is extended
by identical zero in the domain
t<0
with the preserving of the class

C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha}
and of the norm
|f|_{R^{N+1}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}=|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}
.

Further, it is convenient for us for technical reasons to assume that the
extension of the function
f(x,t)
is made in such a way that this function is
the derivative in
t
of a sufficiently high order
n\geq 1
of a finite in
t

function
F(x,t)
with the properties

F(x,t),\frac{\partial^{n}F(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}}\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha}(R^{N+1}),\quad F(x,t)\equiv 0,t<0,

f(x,t)=\frac{\partial^{n}F(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}},(x,t)\in R^{N+1}.

(9.13)

This can be done as follows. Let the support of the extended in
t
on the
whole
R^{N+1}
function
f(x,t)
is included in the domain
R^{N}\times[0,T_{1}]
,
T_{1}>T
. Define
F(x,t)
as
n
-multiple integral in

t
of
f(x,t)
with the subsequent cut-off

F(x,t)\equiv\eta(t){\int\limits_{-1}^{t}}\frac{(t-\tau)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}f(x,\tau)d\tau,t\geq-1,\quad F(x,t)\equiv 0,t<-1,

(9.14)

where

\eta(t)\in C^{\infty}(R^{1}),\quad\eta(t)\equiv 1,t\leq T_{1}+1,\quad\eta(t)\equiv 0,t>T_{1}+1.

In fact,
\partial^{n}F(x,t)/\partial t^{n}
has the somewhat bigger support
in
R^{N}\times[0,T_{1}+1]
than that of the original extended function

f(x,t)
and thus
\partial^{n}F(x,t)/\partial t^{n}
does not coincides with
the original
f(x,t)
for
T_{1}<t<T_{1}+1
. However, since
\partial^{n}F(x,t)/\partial t^{n}=f(x,t)
for
0\leq t\leq T
, we will consider it as
the finite in
t
extension we need of the original function
f(x,t)
.
Properties (
9.13
) follow directly from the way of construction of

F(x,t)
in (
9.14
).

Thus, in what follows we assume that
f(x,t)
is a finite in
t
function,
which is defined on the whole
R^{N+1}=R^{N}\times(-\infty,\infty)
, and it is
the derivative of order
n\geq 1
of some finite in
t
function
F(x,t)
with
the properties in (
9.13
).

9.3
Formulation of the problem in the domain
R^{N+1}=R^{N}\times(-\infty,\infty)
.

Since the function
f(x,t)
is defined in
R^{N+1}
, we can assume that
problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) is the restriction to the interval
[0,T]
of
a similar problem in the domain
R^{N+1}
and we assume, in particular, that

T=\infty
. Besides, in view of zero initial condition and in view of the
conditions
\theta<1
and
\theta\alpha<1
, we can consider the alleged
solution
u(x,t)
to (
9.1
), (
9.2
) to be extended by identical zero
in the domain
\{t<0\}
. Under this extension the function
u(x,t)
preserves
it’s class
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}
, since at

t=0
not only
u(x,0)\equiv 0
, but in the case
\theta+\theta\alpha\in(1,2)

also
u_{t}(x,0)\equiv 0
, as it follows from Proposition
3.3
.
Moreover,
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
, which is defined only for
t>0
, also
can be extended by zero in the domain
t<0
. Such extension preserves the
smoothness not only for
u(x,t)
but for
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
as well,
since this derivative satisfies
\left[D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)\right]|_{t=0}=0
.

Thus problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) can be reformulated as the problem of
finding a function
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(R^{N+1})
, that satisfies in
R^{N+1}
equation (
9.1
)
(with
f(x,t)\equiv 0
for
t<0
)

Lu(x,t)\equiv D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u(x,t)=f(x,t),\quad(x,t)\in R^{N+1}

(9.15)

and that satisfies the condition

u(x,t)\equiv 0,\quad t<0.

(9.16)

9.4
Smoothing of the data .

We are going to find a solution to problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) (and it’s
sharp estimate) as the limit for a sequens of solutions of the class

C^{\infty}(R^{N+1})
to the same problem with finite with respect to all
variables data. Therefore we describe now some smoothing process for the data.
Fix a function (a mollifier kernel) with

\omega(x,t)\in C^{\infty}(R^{N+1}),\quad\omega(x,t)\geq 0,\quad\omega(x,t)\equiv 0,|x|+|t|>1,{\int\limits_{R^{N+1}}}\omega(x,t)dxdt=1,\quad

\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\equiv\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{N+1}}\omega(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{t}{\varepsilon}),\varepsilon>0.

(9.17)

Fix, further, a cut-off function
\zeta(x)
with

\zeta(x)\in C^{\infty}(R^{N}),\quad\zeta(x)\equiv 1,|x|\leq 1,\quad\zeta(x)\equiv 0,|x|>2.

(9.18)

Introduce now the smoothed and cut off with respect to
x
function from
(
9.14
)

F_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\equiv\zeta(\frac{x}{m}){\int\limits_{R^{N+1}}}\omega_{\varepsilon}(x-\xi,t-\tau)F(\xi,\tau-\varepsilon)dxdt,\quad m=1,2,....

(9.19)

Here, in view of properties of
\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
and

F(x,t-\varepsilon)
, the function
F_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
is finite with the
support in
\{|x|\leq 2m\}\times[0,T_{\ast}]
, where
T_{\ast}
is fixed
and does not depend on
m
and
\varepsilon
. By virtue of the known
properties of convolution with a smooth kernel,

F_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\in C^{\infty}(R^{N+1}),

|F_{m,\varepsilon}|_{R^{N+1}}^{(\sigma\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\leq C|F(x,t-\varepsilon)|_{R^{N+1}}^{(\sigma\alpha,\theta\alpha)}=C|F(x,t)|_{R^{N+1}}^{(\sigma\alpha,\theta\alpha)}

(9.20)

and, besides, for each compact set

P_{R}\equiv\{|x|\leq R\}\times\{|t|\leq R\},\quad R>0,

(9.21)

and for an arbitrary
\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha

\left|F_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)-F(x,t)\right|_{P_{R}}^{(\sigma\alpha^{\prime},\theta\alpha^{\prime})}\rightarrow 0,\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0,m\rightarrow\infty.

(9.22)

Further, in view of the properties of the convolution

\frac{\partial^{n}F_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}}=\zeta(\frac{x}{m})\omega_{\varepsilon}\ast\frac{\partial^{n}F(x,t-\varepsilon)}{\partial t^{n}}=

=\zeta(\frac{x}{m}){\int\limits_{R^{N+1}}}\omega_{\varepsilon}(x-\xi,t-\tau)f(\xi,\tau-\varepsilon)dxdt\equiv f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t).

(9.23)

And, analogously to the properties of
F_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
, the function

f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
is finite with it’s support in
\{|x|\leq 2m\}\times[0,T_{\ast}]
,

f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\in C^{\infty}(R^{N+1}),

|f_{m,\varepsilon}|_{R^{N+1}}^{(\sigma\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\leq C|f(x,t-\varepsilon)|_{R^{N+1}}^{(\sigma\alpha,\theta\alpha)}=C|f(x,t)|_{R^{N+1}}^{(\sigma\alpha,\theta\alpha)}.

(9.24)

Moreover, for each compact set
P_{R}
from (
9.21
) and for any

\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha

\left|f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)-f(x,t)\right|_{P_{R}}^{(\sigma\alpha^{\prime},\theta\alpha^{\prime})}\rightarrow 0,\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0,m\rightarrow\infty.

(9.25)

9.5
Constructing a solution in the case of smooth finite data.

Suppose for a while that we are given such a solution
u(x,t)
to
(
9.15
), (
9.16
) from the class
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(R^{N+1})
that it sufficiently rapidly decays
for
t\rightarrow+\infty
and that vanishes for
t<0
. Make in equation
(
9.15
) the Fourier transform and denote the dual variable to
t
by

\xi_{0}
. The Fourier transform of
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
is correctly
defined in view of Lemma
8.4
: we need for that only
u(x,t)\in L_{1}([0,\infty))
for an arbitrary
x\in R^{N}
since in our case
\theta\in(0,1)
. The Fourier transform of the sum of the fractional powers of
Laplace operators we understand in the sense of the space
S^{\prime}(R^{N+1})
since for each
t
we have
u(x,\cdot)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)}(R^{N})\subset S_{\sigma}^{\prime}(R^{N})\subset S^{\prime}(R^{N})
- see Lemma
4.2
. In terms of the Fourier images equation
(
9.15
) takes the form (see (
1.10
), (
1.11
), (
8.24
))

\widehat{Lu}(\xi,\xi_{0})=(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}\widehat{u}(\xi,\xi_{0})+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}\widehat{u}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\widehat{f}(\xi,\xi_{0}),\quad(\xi,\xi_{0})\in R^{N+1}.

(9.26)

Here
\xi=(\xi_{1},...,\xi_{N})
are the dual variables to
x
,
\zeta_{k}=(\xi_{i_{k}+1},...,\xi_{i_{k}+N_{k}})
is group from the whole set
\xi
,
that is the dual group to the group
z_{k}
from
x
. From equality
(
9.15
) it follows that

\widehat{u}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\frac{\widehat{f}(\xi,\xi_{0})}{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}+\widehat{P}(\xi,\xi_{0}),

(9.27)

where
\widehat{P}(\xi,\xi_{0})
is some distribution from
S^{\prime}(R^{N+1})
with the support at the point
(\xi=0,\xi_{0}=0)
, which is a
finite linear combination of the
\delta
-function and it’s derivatives (and
consequently which is the Fourier transform of a polynomial
P(x,t)
).
Consider the first term in the right hand side of (
9.27
) that is the
function

\widehat{u}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{0})\equiv\frac{\widehat{f}(\xi,\xi_{0})}{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}=

=\frac{(i\xi_{0})^{n}}{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}\widehat{F}(\xi,\xi_{0}),

(9.28)

where we took advantage of the fact that

\widehat{f}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\widehat{\frac{\partial^{n}F}{\partial t^{n}}}(\xi,\xi_{0})=(i\xi_{0})^{n}\widehat{F}(\xi,\xi_{0}).

Note first that the function
\widehat{F}(\xi,\xi_{0})
, which is the Fourier
image of a finite function from
C^{\infty}(R^{N+1})
, decays at infinity
faster than any power of
(1+|\xi|+|\xi_{0}|)
. At the same time the function

(i\xi_{0})^{n}/\left[(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}\right]
is bounded at zero and grows at infinity not
faster than
(1+|\xi_{0}|)^{n-\theta}
. Therefore for an arbitrary
M>0

\left|\widehat{u}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{0})\right|\leq C_{M}(1+|\xi|+|\xi_{0}|)^{-M},\quad M>0.

(9.29)

Consequently, there exists the Fourier pre-image of
\widehat{u}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{0})
and thus the corresponding Fourier pre-image
u_{1}(x,t)
belongs
to
C^{\infty}(R^{N+1})
and all derivatives of
u_{1}(x,t)
are bounded on

R^{N+1}
.

Further, in view of the described above properties of
\widehat{F}(\xi,\xi_{0})
, the function
\widehat{u}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{0})
has the derivatives in

\xi_{0}
up to the order
n-1
with the estimate

\left|\frac{\partial^{k}\widehat{u}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{0})}{\partial\xi_{0}^{k}}\right|\leq C_{M}(1+|\xi|+|\xi_{0}|)^{-M},\quad k=0,...,n-1,M>0.

(9.30)

Therefore the function
u_{1}(x,t)
satisfies the estimate

\left|\frac{\partial^{k}u_{1}(x,t)}{\partial t^{k}}\right|\leq C_{k}(1+|t|)^{-(n-1)+k},\quad k=0,...,n-1,

(9.31)

and therefore for
n\geq 3
the function
u_{1}(x,t)
belongs to the space

L_{1}(R^{1})
at each fixed
x
.

At last, denote the factor at
\widehat{F}(\xi,\xi_{0})
in the second
equality in (
9.28
) by

\widehat{G_{n}}(\xi,\xi_{0})\equiv\frac{(i\xi_{0})^{n}}{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}.

(9.32)

This function satisfies all the conditions of Lemma
8.3
and therefore
the support of it’s Fourier pre-image (that is the support of the distribution

G_{n}(x,t)
) is completely included in the set
\{t\geq 0\}
. The same is true
with respect to the function
F(x,t)
by the assumptions. At the same time, by
virtue of the properties of the Fourier transform, from (
9.28
) it
follows that
u_{1}(x,t)
is a convolution of the distribution
G_{n}(x,t)

and the function
F(x,t)\in C^{\infty}(R^{N+1})
,

u_{1}(x,t)=G_{n}(x,t)\ast F(x,t).

Thus, in view of the known properties of convolution

u_{1}(x,t)=0,\quad t<0.

(9.33)

The listed above properties of
u_{1}(x,t)
mean that the operator

Lu_{1}(x,t)
in the left hand side of (
9.15
) is correctly defined on

u_{1}(x,t)
. Besides, the Fourier transforms of all terms in the expression
for
Lu_{1}(x,t)
are also correctly defined (in particular, for each fixed

x\in R^{N}
the function
u_{1}(x,t)
satisfies the conditions of Lemma

8.4
. Finally, the Fourier transform of
Lu_{1}(x,t)
is equal to

\widehat{Lu_{1}}(\xi,\xi_{0})=(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}\widehat{u}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{0})+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}\widehat{u}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\widehat{f}(\xi,\xi_{0})

by virtue of the definition of
u_{1}(x,t)
in (
9.28
). But this means
that
u_{1}(x,t)
satisfies problem (
9.15
), (
9.16
) that is also
problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) with zero initial condition. Thus in equality
(
9.27
)

\widehat{u}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\widehat{u}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{0})+\widehat{P}(\xi,\xi_{0})

the distribution
\widehat{P}(\xi,\xi_{0})
is identically equal to zero,
because
\widehat{u}(\xi,\xi_{0})
and
\widehat{u}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{0})
are the
Fourier images of bounded and vanishing for
t<0
functions, while

\widehat{P}(\xi,\xi_{0})
is the Fourier image of a polynomial in the
variables
x
and
t
. This situation is possible only in the case, when the
polynomial is identically equal to zero.

So, it is shown that for a function
f(x,t)\in C_{0}^{\infty}(R^{N+1})

vanishing for
t<0
there exists a solution
u(x,t)
to problem (
9.15
),
(
9.16
) from the class
C^{\infty}(R^{N+1})
with bounded derivatives of
any order and with some rapid decay for
t\rightarrow\infty
for each fixed

x\in R^{N}
. And as such solution, one can take
u(x,t)=u_{1}(x,t)
, since in
this case

\widehat{u}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\frac{\widehat{f}(\xi,\xi_{0})}{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}.

(9.34)

9.6
Estimate for Hölder norm of solution in the case of smooth
finite data.

We obtain now an estimate of solution from (
9.34
) in the space

C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(R^{N+1})
for infinitely
smooth finite data. To prove such estimate we are going to make use of
representation (
9.34
) and Theorem
8.1
. Consider first the time
derivative
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
. On the ground of (
8.24
) and
(
9.34
) the Fourier transform of this derivative is equal to

\widehat{D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})\widehat{f}(\xi,\xi_{0}),

where

\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})\equiv\frac{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}}{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}.

(9.35)

Thus denoting by
\emph{F}
the Fourier transform with respect to whole set of
the variables
(x,t)
, we have

D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)=\emph{F}^{-1}\left[\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})\emph{F}\left[f(x,t)\right]\right],

(9.36)

that is
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
is obtained from
f(x,t)
by the Fourier
multiplier
\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})
. Verify the properties of

\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})
required by Theorem
8.1
. At first, the

\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})
is evidently bounded on
R^{N+1}
. Further,
according to Theorem
8.1
, split the set of the variables
(\xi,\xi_{0})
into the groups
(\xi,\xi_{0})=(\zeta_{1},...,\zeta_{r},\zeta_{0})
,
where
\zeta_{1}
-
\zeta_{r}
are defined in (
1.10
), (
1.11
) as the
dual for the space groups
z_{k}
,
k=1,...,r
, and
\zeta_{0}=\xi_{0}
is the
dual variable to
t
. The ordered set of the smoothness exponents of
f(x,t)

in the space variables and time is
(\sigma_{1}\alpha,...,\sigma_{r}\alpha,\theta\alpha)
. So in condition (
8.10
) we must consider the
derivatives of the functions (
\lambda>0
)

\widehat{m}_{0}(\lambda^{\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}\alpha}}\zeta_{1},...,\lambda^{\frac{1}{\sigma_{r}\alpha}}\zeta_{r},\lambda^{\frac{1}{\theta\alpha}}\xi_{0})=\frac{(i\lambda^{\frac{1}{\theta\alpha}}\xi_{0})^{\theta}}{(i\lambda^{\frac{1}{\theta\alpha}}\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\lambda^{\frac{1}{\sigma_{k}\alpha}}\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}=

=\frac{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}}{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}=\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0}).

(9.37)

That is condition (
8.10
) must be verified for the function
\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})
itself. Let

p=\frac{1}{1-\delta}>1,\quad\varepsilon\in(0,1),

(9.38)

where
\delta>0
is sufficiently small and will be chosen below. Let further

s_{0}=1
so that according to (
8.8
)

1=s_{0}>\frac{N_{0}}{p}=\frac{1}{p}=1-\delta,

(9.39)

where
N_{0}=1
is the dimension of the group
\zeta_{0}=(\xi_{0})
.
Consequently, it is enough to consider the integrability with the power
p

over the annulus
B_{\nu}\equiv\{(\xi,\xi_{0})\in R^{N+1}:\nu\leq|(\xi,\xi_{0})|\leq\nu^{-1},\nu>0\}
from (
8.9
) of the (possibly mixed)
derivatives of
\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})
, that contains the
differentiation in
\xi_{0}
of order not higher than one. Analogously, choose

s_{k}=N_{k}
,
k=1,...,r
, where
N_{k}
is the dimension of the group

\zeta_{k}
, so that

s_{k}=N_{k}>\frac{N_{k}}{p}=N_{k}(1-\delta).

(9.40)

Therefore it is enough to consider the integrability with the power
p
over
the annulus
B_{\nu}
of the mixed derivatives of
\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})
in the variables of the group
\zeta_{k}
of order not higher than

N_{k}
. At the same time direct simple consideration shows that on
B_{\nu}

\left|D_{\xi_{0}}^{\omega_{0}}D_{\zeta_{1}}^{\omega_{1}}...D_{\zeta_{r}}^{\omega_{r}}\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})\right|\leq C_{\nu,\theta,\overline{\sigma}}|\xi_{0}|^{-1+\theta}{\prod\limits_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{-N_{k}+\sigma_{k}},

(9.41)

under the condition that
\omega_{0}\leq 1=s_{0}
,
|\omega_{k}|\leq N_{k}=s_{k}
. Consequently, we have for the integration of this derivative
with the power
p
over
B_{\nu}

{\int\limits_{B_{\nu}}}\left|D_{\xi_{0}}^{\omega_{0}}D_{\zeta_{1}}^{\omega_{1}}...D_{\zeta_{r}}^{\omega_{r}}\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})\right|^{p}d\xi d\xi_{0}\leq

\leq C_{\nu,\theta,\overline{\sigma}}{\int\limits_{|\xi_{0}|\leq\nu^{-1}}}|\xi_{0}|^{-\frac{1-\theta}{1-\delta}}d\xi_{0}{\prod\limits_{k=1}^{r}\int\limits_{|\zeta_{k}|\leq\nu^{-1}}}|\zeta_{k}|^{-\frac{N_{k}-\sigma_{k}}{1-\delta}}d\zeta_{k}<\infty,

(9.42)

if
\delta>0
chosen sufficiently small to satisfy the conditions

\frac{1-\theta}{1-\delta}<1,\quad\frac{N_{k}-\sigma_{k}}{1-\delta}<N_{k},\quad k=1,...,r.

(9.43)

Thus, under the choice of
\delta>0
from conditions (
9.43
), condition
(
8.10
) is satisfied for the multiplier
\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})

and therefore, according to Theorem
8.1
,

\left\langle D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)\right\rangle_{R^{N+1}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma})\left\langle f(x,t)\right\rangle_{R^{N+1}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}.

(9.44)

Analogously, for the Fourier transform of the fractional Laplace operator

(-\Delta_{z_{i}})^{\frac{\sigma_{i}}{2}}u(x,t)
on the solution
u(x,t)
with
respect to the space group
z_{i}
we have the representation

\widehat{(-\Delta_{z_{i}})^{\frac{\sigma_{i}}{2}}u}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\frac{|\zeta_{i}|^{\sigma_{i}}}{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}\widehat{f}(\xi,\xi_{0})\equiv

(9.45)

\equiv\widehat{m}_{i}(\xi,\xi_{0})\widehat{f}(\xi,\xi_{0}).

The direct verification shows that the multiplier
\widehat{m}_{i}(\xi,\xi_{0})
possesses, similar to
\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})
, all the necessary
properties for the application of Theorem
8.1
, including estimates
(
9.41
) and (
9.42
) with the same
p>1
. Consequently

\left\langle(-\Delta_{z_{i}})^{\frac{\sigma_{i}}{2}}u(x,t)\right\rangle_{R^{N+1}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma})\left\langle f(x,t)\right\rangle_{R^{N+1}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}.

(9.46)

Based now on (
9.44
), (
9.46
) and making use of Proposition

3.3
and of estimate (
3.11
), we get

\left\langle u(x,t)\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)},

(9.47)

where we suppose that either the support of
f(x,t)
is included in

\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
or the norm of
f(x,t)
on it’s compact support is
estimated by it’s norm over
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
(as it is the case after
the extension of
f(x,t)
out of
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
). Besides, we are
considering now the restriction of the solution
u(x,t)
from
R^{N+1}
to

\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
.

Further we note that estimate (
9.47
) for the highest seminorm of

u(x,t)
does not depend on the dimensions of the the support of
f(x,t)
and
it is defined only by the total norm
\left|f(x,t)\right|_{R^{N+1}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}
over
R^{N+1}
. At the same time
the lowest norm
|u(x,t)|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(0)}
depends on
T
in
general . The simplest estimate for this norm follows from the fact that

u(x,0)\equiv 0
, from the last assertion of Proposition
3.3
, from the
contained in (
9.47
) estimate

\left\langle u(x,t)\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)},

and from from the definition of the Hölder seminorm with respect to
t

itself. Such estimate takes place for an arbitrary
\widetilde{T}\leq T

|u(x,t)|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq\left\langle u(x,t)\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(\theta+\theta\alpha)}\widetilde{T}^{\theta+\theta\alpha}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\widetilde{T}^{\theta+\theta\alpha},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq T.

(9.48)

Combining (
9.47
) and (
9.48
) we arrive at the full estimate of
the norm

\left|u(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma},T)\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}=C(\theta,\overline{\sigma},T)\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}.

(9.49)

9.7
Existence of a solution for an arbitrary
f(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
.

Let now
f(x,t)
be an arbitrary function from
C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with
f(x,0)\equiv 0
. Consider the
sequence of it’s smooth finite approximations
f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
that
was constructed in section
9.4
and which satisfies (
9.24
),
(
9.25
). In sections
9.5
and
9.6
it was shown that for each

f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
there exists a solution
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
to
problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) (with
u_{0}(x)\equiv 0
), which satisfies
estimate (
9.49
) that is

\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma},T)\left|f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\leq

\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma},T)\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}.

(9.50)

Or, more precisely,

\left\langle u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)},

(9.51)

|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\widetilde{T}^{\theta+\theta\alpha},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq T.

(9.52)

From the know properties of Hölder spaces (see
[
40
]
, the end part of
the proof to Theorem 2.1 on page 404) it follows that there exists a function

u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with

\left|u(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma},T)\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)},

(9.53)

\left\langle u(x,t)\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)},

(9.54)

|u(x,t)|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\theta,\overline{\sigma})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\widetilde{T}^{\theta+\theta\alpha},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq T,

(9.55)

and for each compact set

B_{R,T}\equiv\{(x,t)\in\overline{R_{T}^{N}}:|x|\leq R,\quad t\in[0,T]\},\quad R>0,

and each
\alpha^{\prime}\in(0,\alpha)

\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)-u(x,t)\right|_{B_{R,T}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha^{\prime},\theta+\theta\alpha^{\prime})}\rightarrow 0,\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0,m\rightarrow\infty,

(9.56)

at least for a subsequence. Relations (
9.50
) - (
9.56
) permit to
perform a limiting process in equation (
9.1
). Indeed, from this
relations it follows, in particular, that for a fixed
t\in[0,T]
the
sequence (subsequence)
\{u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\}\subset S_{\overline{\sigma}}^{\prime}(R^{N})\subset S^{\prime}(R^{N})
is included in the class

S_{\overline{\sigma}}^{\prime}(R^{N})
, which was defined in (
4.16
)
and this sequence satisfies the conditions of Proposition
4.4
.
Consequently, this sequens converges to
u(x,t)
in
S^{\prime}(R^{N})
for a
fixed
t\in[0,T]
,

u_{m,\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\rightarrow_{S^{\prime}(R^{N})}u(\cdot,t),

{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u_{m,\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\rightarrow_{S^{\prime}(R^{N})}{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u(\cdot,t),\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0,m\rightarrow\infty,

(9.57)

where
S_{\overline{\sigma}}^{\prime}(R^{N})
is defined in (
4.16
).
Further, Proposition
3.1
, applied to the sequence
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
uniformly in
x\in R^{N}
, together with (
9.56
) means, in
particular, the convergence

\max_{x\in B_{R,T}}\left|D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,\cdot)-D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,\cdot)\right|_{[0,T]}^{(\theta\alpha^{\prime})}\rightarrow 0,\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0,m\rightarrow\infty,\quad x\in R^{N}.

(9.58)

Consequently, the more, for a fixed
t\in[0,T]
,

D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u_{m,\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\rightarrow_{S^{\prime}(R^{N})}D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(\cdot,t).

(9.59)

Thus, taking also into account (
9.25
), we can perform the limiting
process in equation (
9.1
) for a fixed
t\in[0,T]
in space

S^{\prime}(R^{N})
for the functions
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
and

f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
. This means that that for a fixed
t\in[0,T]

the limit function
u(x,t)
satisfies the equation in the sense of
S^{\prime}(R^{N})
. But the function
u(x,t)
belongs to the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
and all the
fractional differential operators in the left hand side of (
9.1
) are
defined in the usual sense (see section
6
). Therefore
u(x,t)
is a
solution from the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
to problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) with

u_{0}(x)\equiv 0
, which satisfies estimate (
9.53
). Since the general
case with
u_{0}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)}(R^{N})
can be reduced
to zero initial data, as it was shown in section
9.1
, then we’ve proved
the following assertion.

Proposition 9.2

If conditions (
9.3
) - (
9.6
) are satisfied, then
problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) has a solution
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with estimates
(
9.7
) - (
9.9
).

Formulate now a corollary of this proposition about the existence of a global
solution to problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) for all
t>0
, that is for

T=\infty
, on infinite interval
(0,\infty)
.

Corollary 9.3

Let for problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) with
T=\infty

conditions (
9.3
) - (
9.6
) are satisfied and the given function

f(x,t)
is defined for all
t>0
in the domain
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}=R^{N}\times[0,\infty)
and it’s norm in the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
is finite that is

\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}<\infty
. Then problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) has a
solution
u(x,t)
, which locally in
t
belongs to the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
and which
satisfies estimates (
9.8
), (
9.9
) that is

\left\langle u\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

(9.60)

\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right)\widetilde{T}^{\theta+\theta\alpha}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(0)},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq\infty.

(9.61)

Consequently, for an arbitrary finite
T>0
estimate (
9.7
) is also valid.

Proof.
From Proposition
9.2
it follows that for an arbitrary
T>0
there
exists a solution
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
to problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) in
the domain
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
with properties in (
9.7
) -
(
9.9
). Consider a sequence
\{T_{n}\}
,
n=1,2,...
,
T_{n+1}>T_{n}>0
,
T_{n}\rightarrow\infty
,
n\rightarrow\infty
. Denote by
u_{n}(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T_{n}}^{N}})

a corresponding solution to (
9.1
), (
9.2
) in the domain

\overline{R_{T_{n}}^{N}}
. We stress at this point that we don’t have an
assertion on the uniqueness of the solution to (
9.1
), (
9.2
) in the
domains
\overline{R_{T_{n}}^{N}}
, therefore we can not assert that for

m>n
the solution
u_{n}
in the domain
\overline{R_{T_{n}}^{N}}
coincides
with the restriction of the solution
u_{m}
in the domain
\overline{R_{T_{m}}^{N}}
to the domain
\overline{R_{T_{n}}^{N}}
,
T_{n}<T_{m}
.

Consider the first number (index)
n=1
and consider the solutions
u_{m}
in
the more wider domains
\overline{R_{T_{m}}^{N}}
,
m>1
. On the ground of
estimates (
9.8
), (
9.9
) we conclude that for the all numbers

m>1
we have estimates (
9.7
) - (
9.9
) in the domain

\overline{R_{T_{1}}^{N}}
for
u_{m}
