|u_{m}|_{\overline{R_{T_{1}}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha,T_{1})\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),\quad m>1,

(9.62)

\left\langle u_{m}\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T_{1}}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T_{1}}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),\quad m>1,

(9.63)

\left|u_{m}\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T_{1}}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right)\widetilde{T}^{\theta+\theta\alpha}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(0)},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq T_{1},m>1.

(9.64)

Consequently, as it was in the proof of Proposition
9.2
, there exists
such a function
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T_{1}}^{N}})
, together with a subsequence
\{u_{m_{k}}^{(1)},k=1,2,...\}\subset\{u_{m},m>1\}
, that the sequence
\{u_{m_{k}}^{(1)},k=1,2,...\}
converges to
u(x,t)
in the sense of (
9.56
) -
(
9.59
). And at that
u(x,t)
is a solution to (
9.1
), (
9.2
)
in the domain
\overline{R_{T_{1}}^{N}}
with estimates (
9.62
) -
(
9.64
). Consider now the sequence
\{u_{m_{k}}^{(1)}\}
in the domain

\overline{R_{T_{2}}^{N}}
for the numbers
k
with
m_{k}>2
, so that all the
functions
u_{m_{k}}^{(1)}
are defined in the domain
\overline{R_{T_{2}}^{N}}
. Exactly as it was at the first step, we choose from the sequence

\{u_{m_{k}}^{(1)}\}
such a subsequence
\{u_{m_{k}}^{(2)},k=1,2,...\}
, that
converges in the sense of (
9.56
) - (
9.59
) to a solution of
(
9.1
), (
9.2
) already in the wider domain
\overline{R_{T_{2}}^{N}}
. We denote the obtained solution by the same symbol
u(x,t)
since on the
included domain
\overline{R_{T_{1}}^{N}}\subset\overline{R_{T_{2}}^{N}}
the
limit remains the same after going to a subsequence. Proceeding with this
process, we obtain a countable set of included subsequences
\{u_{m_{k}}^{(1)}\}\supset\{u_{m_{k}}^{(2)}\}\supset...\supset\{u_{m_{k}}^{(n)}\}\supset....
. Each of these subsequences converges in the sense of
(
9.56
) - (
9.59
) to the same solution
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}
to (
9.1
), (
9.2
) with
properties (
9.60
), (
9.61
) in the corresponding expanding domains

\overline{R_{T_{n}}^{N}}
,
n=1,2,...,n,...
. Choosing now from this set of
the subsequences a diagonal subsequence, we see that the last converges,
starting with the corresponding index, on each compact set in
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}\equiv R^{N}\times[0,\infty)
to the solution
u(x,t)

to (
9.1
), (
9.2
) for
T=\infty
. And it is readily verified by the
construction that the obtained solution
u(x,t)
possesses properties
(
9.60
), (
9.61
) in the whole domain
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}
,
which finishes the proof.

9.8
Extension of a solution to the whole interval
t\in(0,\infty)
.

In the present section we consider the issue of an extension of a solution
from the finite time interval to the whole time half-axis. This consideration
plays an auxiliary role. We need such considerations at this stage because we
don’t have for a while an assertion on the uniqueness of the solution from the
class
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
on a time interval
[0,T]
. Therefore, obtaining a solution on
the whole time half-axis on the ground of Corollary
9.3
, we can not
assert for a while that this solution is the extension of a given solution on
a finite interval
[0,T]
. The uniqueness of the solution will be proved in
the following section
9.9
based on an extension and with an application
of the Fourier transform.

Let the right hand side
f(x,t)
in (
9.1
) is defined (or extended from
the finite interval
[0,T]
) on
t\in(0,\infty)
in the way that it belongs on
the whole
t\in(0,\infty)
to the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
with a possibly reduced exponent
\beta\leq\alpha
with
\theta+\theta\beta<1
(note that
f(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})\subset C^{\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
). We will show that then a solution

u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
to (
9.1
), (
9.2
) on a finite interval
[0,T]
can be
extended up to a solution to (
9.1
), (
9.2
) on the whole time
half-axis with estimates in the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
analogously to
(
9.60
), (
9.61
) from Corollary
9.3
(with the replacing

\alpha
with
\beta
).

We first prove an auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 9.4

Let
\theta,\beta>0
be nonintegers,
\{\theta\}+\theta\beta\in(0,1)
. Let further a function
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
,
T>0
and let at
t=0

it satisfy the conditions

u_{t}^{(k)}(x,0)\equiv 0,\quad k=0,1,...,[\theta].

(9.65)

Let, besides, this function be extended on the whole
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}
to the domain
t>T
by the Taylor polynomial in
t
of power
[\theta]

at the point
t=T
that is

u(x,t)={\sum\limits_{k=0}^{[\theta]}}u_{t}^{(k)}(x,T)\frac{(t-T)^{k}}{k!},\quad t\geq T.

(9.66)

Then there exists the bounded for
t>0
fractional Caputo - Jrbashyan
derivative
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
of the extended function and it belongs
to the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
. Moreover,

\left|D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta)}\leq C(\theta,\beta,T)\left|u(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta)}.

(9.67)

Proof.
Note first that after the extension (
9.66
) the highest possible integer
derivative
u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)
is continuous
t=T
(along with all
derivatives in
t
of a less order). Besides, in view of the condition

\{\theta\}+\theta\beta<1
, which means the absence of the next integer
derivative
u_{t}^{([\theta]+1)}(x,t)
,
u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)
retains in
the whole domain
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}
the smoothness class inherited
from the subdomain
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
. As it follows from (
2.6
),

u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)\in C^{\frac{\{\theta\}+\theta\beta}{\theta+\theta\beta}\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\{\theta\}+\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
. At that, since
u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)\equiv u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,T)
for all
t\geq T
, then

\left|u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{\left(\frac{\{\theta\}+\theta\beta}{\theta+\theta\beta}\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\{\theta\}+\theta\beta\right)}\leq C(\theta,\beta)\left|u(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta)}.

(9.68)

Make use of the representation for
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
from the second
equality in (
1.9
). That is, bearing in mind (
9.65
),

D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\theta)}\frac{d}{dt}{\int\limits_{0}^{t}}\frac{u_{\tau}^{([\theta])}(x,\tau)d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{\{\theta\}}}.

(9.69)

From this representation it follows that in fact
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)

coincides (up to a constant factor) with the Caputo - Jrbashyan derivative in

t
of order
\{\theta\}
of the function
u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)
(we remind
condition (
9.65
)). This derivative is defined correctly since

u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)
has the smoothness in
t
of order
\{\theta\}+\theta\beta
. Applying Theorem
6.9
, we conclude that on the time
interval
[0,3T]
(that is in the domain
\overline{R_{3T}^{N}}
) the
following estimate is valid

\left|D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u\right|_{\overline{R_{3T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta)}\leq C(\theta,\beta,T)\left|u_{t}^{([\theta])}\right|_{\overline{R_{3T}^{N}}}^{\left(\frac{\{\theta\}+\theta\beta}{\theta+\theta\beta}\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\{\theta\}+\theta\beta\right)}\leq

\leq C(\theta,\beta,T)\left|u(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta)}.

(9.70)

Let now
t>2T
. Taking into account that
u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)\equiv u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,T)
for
t\geq T
, represent the derivative
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u
from (
9.69
) in the form of the sum

D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)=C(\theta)\frac{d}{dt}\left[{\int\limits_{0}^{T}}\frac{u_{\tau}^{([\theta])}(x,\tau)d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{\{\theta\}}}+{\int\limits_{T}^{t}}\frac{u_{\tau}^{([\theta])}(x,\tau)d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{\{\theta\}}}\right]=

=C(\theta){\int\limits_{0}^{T}}\frac{u_{\tau}^{([\theta])}(x,\tau)d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{1+\{\theta\}}}+C(\theta)u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,T)\frac{d}{dt}{\int\limits_{T}^{t}}\frac{d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{\{\theta\}}}=

=C(\theta){\int\limits_{0}^{T}}\frac{u_{\tau}^{([\theta])}(x,\tau)d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{1+\{\theta\}}}+C(\theta)u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,T)(t-T)^{-\{\theta\}}\equiv I_{1}(x,t)+I_{2}(x,t).

Here for
t>2T
the integral
I_{1}(x,t)
does not have even weak singularity
and it is, in fact, infinitely differentiable in
t
with the preserving of
the smoothness in
x
of
u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)
. The same is valid with
respect to
I_{2}(x,t)
as well. Therefore, due to the estimate for

u_{t}^{([\theta])}(x,t)
in (
9.68
), we the more have for
t>2T

\left|D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u\right|_{R^{N}\times[2T,\infty)}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta)}\leq C(\theta,\beta,T)\left|u(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta)}.

(9.71)

Combining (
9.70
) and (
9.71
) we arrive at the assertion of the lemma.

Proposition 9.5

Let us be given a solution
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
to (
9.1
),
(
9.2
), which is defined in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
. Let also the right
hand side
f(x,t)
in (
9.1
) be defined (or extended from
[0,T]
) for
all
t>0
in the domain
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}
and let
f(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
with such

\beta\leq\alpha
that
\theta+\theta\beta<1
. Then there exists a solution to
(
9.1
), (
9.2
) in the domain
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}
(which is
denoted by the same symbol
u(x,t)
). This solution belongs to the space

C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}})
for any
\widetilde{T}>0
, it coincides with
u(x,t)
in

\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
, and it obeys in
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}
to the
analogous to (
9.60
), (
9.61
) estimates

\left\langle u\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\beta)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta)}+\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta+\theta\beta)}\right),

(9.72)

\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta)}+\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta+\theta\beta)}\right)\widetilde{T}^{\theta+\theta\beta}+\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta+\theta\beta)},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq\infty.

(9.73)

Proof.
Note first that, as it was shown in section
9.1
, we can assume without
loss of generality that we are given zero initial data in (
9.2
) that is

u(x,0)=u_{0}(x)\equiv 0
. Consider in the domain
R_{T,\infty}^{N}\equiv R^{N}\times[T,\infty)
the following Cauchy problem for the unknown
function, which is denoted by the same symbol
u(x,t)
,

Lu(x,t)\equiv D_{T,\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u(x,t)=f(x,t)-D_{\ast t}^{\theta}\widetilde{u}(x,t),\quad(x,t)\in R_{T,T_{1}}^{N},

(9.74)

u(x,T)=u_{T}(x),\quad x\in R^{N}.

(9.75)

Here
D_{T,\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
is the Kaputo - Jrbashyan derivative of

u(x,t)
with the starting point
t=T

D_{T,\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)\equiv\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\theta)}\frac{d}{dt}{\int\limits_{T}^{t}}\frac{\left[u(x,\tau)-u(x,T)\right]d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{\theta}}=

=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\theta)}\frac{d}{dt}{\int\limits_{T}^{t}}\frac{\left[u(x,\tau)-u_{T}(x)\right]d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{\theta}},

(9.76)

the function
\widetilde{u}(x,t)
is the extension of the given in

\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
solution
u(x,t)
to the domain
t>T
by the Taylor
polynomial of zero order in
t
(that is by the corresponding constant in

t
)

\widetilde{u}(x,t)\equiv\genfrac{}{.}{0.0pt}{0}{u(x,t),\quad t\in\lbrack 0,T],}{u(x,T),\quad t\geq T,}

(9.77)

and
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}\widetilde{u}(x,t)
is the Caputo - Jrbashyan
derivative of
\widetilde{u}(x,t)
with the starting point
t=0

D_{\ast t}^{\theta}\widetilde{u}(x,t)\equiv\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\theta)}\frac{d}{dt}{\int\limits_{0}^{t}}\frac{\widetilde{u}(x,\tau)d\tau}{(t-\tau)^{\theta}},

(9.78)

since by assumption
\widetilde{u}(x,0)=u(x,0)\equiv 0
.

We are going to apply to problem (
9.74
), (
9.75
) Corollary

9.3
on the solvability. Note first that the difference of the starting
point
t=T
from the case
t=0
in Corollary
9.3
is not essential. This
case is trivially reduced to the starting point
t=0
by the time change

t=\overline{t}+T
. Therefore, it is enough to verify the conditions of
Corollary
9.3
for problem (
9.74
), (
9.75
).

Firstly,
f(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
by assumption and the more
f(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{T,\infty}^{N}})
. Further, the function

\widetilde{u}(x,t)
from (
9.77
) belongs to the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})\subset C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
.
Therefore, from Lemma
9.4
it follows that

\left|D_{\ast t}^{\theta}\widetilde{u}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta)}\leq C\left|u(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta+\theta\beta)}.

(9.79)

At last, verify compatibility condition (
9.6
) at
t=T
. Indeed,
according with this condition we must have the equality

{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u(x,T)=f(x,T)-D_{\ast t}^{\theta}\widetilde{u}(x,t)|_{t=T}.

But at
t=T
we have by definition
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}\widetilde{u}(x,t)|_{t=T}=D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)|_{t=T}
and the equality holds in
view of the fact that
u(x,t)
is a solution to (
9.1
) in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
. Therefore, on the ground of Corollary
9.3
, we infer that
there exists a solution
u(x,t)
to problem (
9.74
), (
9.75
) with
estimates (
9.72
), (
9.73
).

Show now that the originally defined in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
function

u(x,t)
, which is defined already in the whole
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}\text{ }}
, satisfies equation (
9.1
) totally in
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}\text{ }}
. Note that in view of initial condition
(
9.75
) and the condition
\theta+\theta\beta<1
the function
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}})
for an arbitrary
\widetilde{T}>0
. Further, for
t\leq T
the
function
u(x,t)
satisfies equation (
9.1
) and initial condition
(
9.2
) by assumption. Let now
t\geq T
. Moving the expression
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}\widetilde{u}(x,t)
from the right hand side of equation
(
9.74
) to it’s left hand side and taking into account (
9.76
) and
definition (
9.77
), we see that for
t\geq T

D_{T,\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)+D_{\ast t}^{\theta}\widetilde{u}(x,t)=D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t),

that is equation (
9.1
) is satisfied for all
t>T
. Since
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}})
for an arbitrary
\widetilde{T}>0
and the operators
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}
,
(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}
in (
9.1
) are
continuous from the pointed space to the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}})
, equation (
9.1
) is
satisfied by continuity at
t=T
as well. And this finishes the proof.

9.9
Uniqueness of solution and finishing the proof of Theorem

9.1
.

On this step we prove the uniqueness of the obtained in Proposition
9.2

solution from the class
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
.

Lemma 9.6

Let a function
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
satisfy the homogeneous problem
(
9.1
), (
9.2
) with
f(x,t)\equiv 0
and
u_{0}(x)\equiv 0
. Then

u(x,t)\equiv 0
in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
.

Proof.
Make use of Proposition
9.5
and extend the given solution
u(x,t)
for
all
t>0
to a solution (with the same symbol
u(x,t)
) to the homogeneous
problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) in the whole domain
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}
. It is possible since in our case
f(x,t)\equiv 0
on
t\in[0,T]

and it can be extended by identical zero to
t\in[0,\infty)
. The
obtained solution
u(x,t)
can be also extended by identical zero in the
domain
t<0
with the preserving of the smoothness (in view of zero initial
data and in view of (
3.6
)). Besides, this extended solution has, in
view of (
9.73
), a power growth for
t\rightarrow\infty
. Consequently,
we can consider this function as an element of the space of distributions

\Phi^{\prime}(R^{N+1})
from section
5
. Finally, the extended by zero
in the domain
t<0
function
u(x,t)
satisfies equation (
9.1
) on the
whole space
R^{N+1}
.

Apply the Fourier transform according to formulas (
5.3
), (
5.4
)
to equation (
9.1
) for the function
u(x,t)
under consideration. We
obtain

\left[(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}\right]\widehat{u}(\xi,\xi_{0})=0,\quad(\xi,\xi_{0})\in R^{N+1}.

From this it follows that
\widehat{u}(\xi,\xi_{0})
is a distribution from

\Psi^{\prime}(R^{N+1})
with the support at the point
(\xi,\xi_{0})=(0,0)

that is, according to the definition of the space
\Psi^{\prime}(R^{N+1})
,
this distribution represents the zero class of
\Psi^{\prime}(R^{N+1})
. But
this means that the function
u(x,t)
itself represents the zero class of the
space
\Phi^{\prime}(R^{N+1})
that is
u(x,t)
is a polynomial. But since

u(x,t)
identically vanishes for
t<0
, then
u(x,t)\equiv 0
in
R^{N+1}

and, in particular, in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
. This finishes the proof.

On the ground of the proved lemma the uniqueness of solution to problem
(
9.1
), (
9.2
) in the class
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
follows now from linearity of
problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
).

Thus, Proposition
9.2
together with Lemma
9.6
finish the proof
of Theorem
9.1
.

10
A Cauchy problem for equation (
1.2
) in the case of the integer

\theta=1
,
\theta\alpha=\alpha\in(0,1)
.

In this section we consider solvability of problem (
1.2
), (
1.3
) in
the anisotropic Hölder spaces
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
from (
2.8
) in the case of the integer
derivative in time of order
\theta=1
. Consider the following Cauchy problem
for the defined in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
unknown function
u(x,t)

Lu(x,t)\equiv u_{t}(x,t)+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u(x,t)=f(x,t),\quad(x,t)\in R_{T}^{N},

(10.1)

u(x,0)=u_{0}(x),\quad x\in R^{N},

(10.2)

where the given functions belongs to the spaces

f(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}}),\quad u_{0}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)}(R^{N}).

(10.3)

In the present section we first suppose that
\alpha
is sufficiently small

\quad\alpha\in(0,1),\quad 0<\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\overline{\sigma},\{N_{k}\}),

(10.4)

where the particular value of
\alpha_{0}\in(0,1)
will be given below. On the
later steps in this section the second restriction in (
10.4
) will be
removed. We stress also that for an integer derivative in
t
of order

\theta
a compatibility condition of the kind (
9.6
) is not necessary.

The main assertion of the present section is as follows.

Theorem 10.1

If
\alpha\in(0,1)
and conditions (
10.3
) are satisfied,
then problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) has the unique solution
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with the
estimates

|u|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,T)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

(10.5)

\left\langle u\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

(10.6)

\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right)(1+\widetilde{T}^{1+\alpha})+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(0)},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq T.

(10.7)

Analogously to the previous section, we give the proof of the above theorem
into several steps, which is the subsequent content of the present section.

10.1
Reduction of the problem to zero initial conditions.

Similar to the previous section we can assume from the very beginning zero
initial data

u_{0}(x)\equiv 0,\quad x\in R^{N},

(10.8)

which is achieved by the change of the unknown in problem (
10.1
),
(
10.2
)

u(x,t)\rightarrow v(x,t)=u(x,t)-u_{0}(x).

The new unknown function
v(x,t)
satisfies initial condition (
10.2
) and
the equation

v_{t}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}v=\widetilde{f(}x,t)\equiv f(x,t)-{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u_{0}(x).

From Proposition
6.3
it follows that
\widetilde{f(}x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
like the original
function
f(x,t)
, and besides

|\widetilde{f}|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.9)

We stress that in contrast to the case of fractional time derivative

\theta\in(0,1)
in previous section, now we do not have a compatibility
condition of the kind (
9.6
). Therefore we can not assert the validity of
the condition
\widetilde{f(}x,0)\equiv 0
. This fact is the main difference
from the previous case, since this does not permit to extend
\widetilde{f(}x,t)
by zero for
t<0
with the preserving of the smoothness in
t
.
Therefore in the present section, to estimate the highest seminorm in the
corresponding Hölder space, we make use Theorem
8.2
on Fourier
multipliers instead of Theorem
8.1
. Theorem
8.2
permits us to
perform the necessary estimates without assuming the smoothness of

\widetilde{f(}x,t)
for all
t
. But this causes some technical restrictions
for the Hölder exponent
\alpha
(see the last condition in (
10.4
))
and therefore some additional consideration then needed to remove these restrictions.

Thus, below we assume zero initial conditions that is (
10.8
).

Remark 10.2

Here the following should be noted. There exists a method of the
extension of the initial data
u_{0}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)}(R^{N})
to the domain
t>0
up to a function
U_{0}(x,t)
from the space

C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
that not only
retains the initial value
U_{0}(x,0)=u_{0}(x)
but also the initial value of
the derivative in time of the solution itself
\partial U_{0}(x,0)/\partial t=u_{1}(x)\equiv\partial u(x,0)/\partial t
. This last is prescribed by the
equation (
10.1
) with taking into account condition (
10.2
). If one
has such a function
U_{0}(x,t)
, one can make the change of the unknown

u(x,t)\rightarrow u(x,t)-U_{0}(x,t)
and the original problem can be reduced
to the case, when one has not only zero initial data but also
\widetilde{f(}x,0)\equiv 0
. Such method is described in, for example,
[
35
]
.
However, according to this method the auxiliary function
U_{0}(x,t)
is
constructed as a solution to the simplest model parabolic equation with
integer derivatives and with the initial data
u_{0}(x)
and
u_{1}(x)
. The
properties of Cauchy problems for the mentioned simplest model equation (heat
equation, for example) are supposed to be well known in advance. The
application of the method from
[
35
]
permits to obtain such extension

U_{0}(x,t)
for our case as well, but only when all the orders of fractional
differentiation
\sigma_{k}
in
x
are rational. But for the case when at
least on of the orders
\sigma_{k}
is irrational this method does not appear
to be applicable. The reason is that equation (
10.1
) is precisely that
simplest model equation for the case of fractional differentiation and our
purpose is precisely to describe it’s properties in the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
. This causes the necessity
to apply below Theorem
8.2
on Fourier multipliers in Hölder spaces
with some partial Hölder regularity.

10.2
Extension and smoothing of data, formulation of the problem in

R^{N+1}=R^{N}\times(-\infty,\infty)
.

In this section we, analogously to sections
9.2
,
9.3
, assume
zero initial data
u(x,0)\equiv 0
and assume that the righthand side
f(x,t)

is extended in
t
to the domain
t>T
up to a finite in
t
function with the
class preservation. Besides, we assume that
f(x,t)
is extended to
t<0
by
identical zero. As a result, we have a finite in
t
function (which is
denoted by the same symbol
f(x,t)
) and this function is defined in the whole

R^{N+1}
. The extended
f(x,t)
has (in general) a jump at
t=0
and it has
the properties

|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)},\quad|f|_{R^{N+1}}^{(0)}\leq C|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(0)},\quad f(x,t)\equiv 0,t<0.

(10.10)

That is
f(x,t)
has a controlled Hölder norm in the upper half-space only
but it has a controlled bound in the whole space.

Further, as it was in section
9.2
, we assume that
f(x,t)
is an
integer derivative in
t
of a sufficiently high order
n
of a finite in
t

function
F(x,t)
with

F(x,t),\frac{\partial^{n}F(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}}\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}),\quad\frac{\partial^{n-1}F(x,t)}{\partial t^{n-1}}\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha}(R^{N+1}),

F(x,t)\equiv 0,t<0,\quad f(x,t)=\frac{\partial^{n}F(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}},(x,t)\in R^{N+1}.

(10.11)

The way to construct such a function
F(x,t)
coincides with that from section

9.2
.

We apply also to the functions
F(x,t)
and
f(x,t)
the smoothing by the
convolution with the mollifier
\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
from (
9.17
),
and after that we cut them off up to finite in
x
functions
F_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
and
f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
from
C^{\infty}(R^{N+1})
. This process
is almost replicates the process from (
9.18
), (
9.19
) but with a
tiny difference. In the present situation we put

F_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\equiv\zeta(\frac{x}{m}){\int\limits_{R^{N+1}}}\omega_{\varepsilon}(x-\xi,t-\tau)F(\xi,\tau+\varepsilon)dxdt,\quad m=1,2,...,

(10.12)

and correspondingly

f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\equiv\zeta(\frac{x}{m}){\int\limits_{R^{N+1}}}\omega_{\varepsilon}(x-\xi,t-\tau)f(\xi,\tau+\varepsilon)dxdt,\quad m=1,2,....

(10.13)

We stress that the difference is that the density
F(\xi,\tau+\varepsilon)

and
f(\xi,\tau+\varepsilon)
of the convolutions are shifted by
\varepsilon

in the negative direction of the
t
-axis, in contrast to (
9.19
). The
aim of this is to move the possible jump of
f(x,t)
from the point
t=0
to
the point
t=-\varepsilon
and thus to provide the uniform regularity of the
mollified functions in the half-space
\{t\geq 0\}
for all
\varepsilon
. At
that, identically to (
9.23
),

\frac{\partial^{n}F_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}}\equiv f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t),\quad(x,t)\in R^{N+1}.

(10.14)

Remind that the function
f(x,t)
has, in general, a jump at
t=0
, and
therefore the smoothed functions
f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
possess more weak
uniform properties than those in (
9.24
), (
9.25
). Namely,

|f_{m,\varepsilon}|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\sigma\alpha,\alpha)}\leq C|f(x,t+\varepsilon)|_{R^{N}\times[-\varepsilon,\infty)}^{(\sigma\alpha,\alpha)}=C|f(x,t)|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\sigma\alpha,\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}

(10.15)

and moreover, for each compact
P_{R}^{+}\equiv P_{R}\cap\{t\geq 0\}
, where

P_{R}
is defined in (
9.21
), and for each
\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha

\left|f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)-f(x,t)\right|_{P_{R}^{+}}^{(\sigma\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})}\rightarrow 0,\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0,m\rightarrow\infty.

(10.16)

Besides, in the whole space
R^{N+1}
the functions
f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)

have the following properties (
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
)

f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\in C^{\infty}(R^{N+1}),

f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\equiv 0,t<-2\varepsilon,\quad|f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)|_{R^{N+1}}^{(0)}\leq C|f(x,t)|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(0)},

(10.17)

\left\langle f_{m,\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{x,R^{N+1}}^{(\sigma\alpha)}\leq C|f(x,t-\varepsilon)|_{R^{N}\times[-\varepsilon,\infty)}^{(\sigma\alpha,\alpha)}=

=C|f(x,t)|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\sigma\alpha,\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)},

(10.18)

\left\langle f_{m,\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{t,R^{N+1}}^{(\alpha)}\leq C\varepsilon^{-\alpha}|f(x,t-\varepsilon)|_{R^{N}\times[-\varepsilon,\infty)}^{(\sigma\alpha,\alpha)}=

=C\varepsilon^{-\alpha}|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\sigma\alpha,\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\varepsilon^{-\alpha}|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}.

(10.19)

That is the finite functions
f_{m,\varepsilon}
have uniformly bounded
maximum of modulus over the whole space
R^{N+1}
and the uniformly bounded
Hölder seminorms in
x
over the whole space
R^{N+1}
, as it is stated in
(
10.17
), (
10.18
). This facts easy follows from the definitions of

f_{m,\varepsilon}
with taking into account that the extended by zero to

t<0
original function
f(x,t)
does not have jumps in directions of
x
-
variables. The last property in (
10.19
) asserts that the Hölder
seminorms in
t
of the smoothed functions
f_{m,\varepsilon}
over the whole

R^{N+1}
have the order
\varepsilon^{-\alpha}
for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. This follows from from definitions (
9.17
), (
10.13
) and it is
because the extended function
f(x,t)
may have a jump in
t
at
t=0
. For
the completeness we present a simple auxiliary lemma, which is a direct ground
for (
10.19
).

Lemma 10.3

Let
f(x,t)\in L_{\infty}(R^{N+1})
and let
\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
be defined in (
9.17
). Then

\left\langle f_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{t,R^{N+1}}^{(\alpha)}\leq C\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\left\|f\right\|_{L_{\infty}(R^{N+1})},

where

f_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\equiv{\int\limits_{R^{N+1}}}\omega_{\varepsilon}(x-\xi,t-\tau)f(\xi,\tau)dxdt.

Proof.
On the ground of the definition of
f_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
, consider the
difference

D(\Delta t)\equiv\left|\frac{f_{\varepsilon}(x,t+\Delta t)-f_{\varepsilon}(x,t)}{(\Delta t)^{\alpha}}\right|=

=\left|{\int\limits_{R^{N+1}}}\frac{\omega_{\varepsilon}(x-\xi,t+\Delta t-\tau)-\omega_{\varepsilon}(x-\xi,t-\tau)}{(\Delta t)^{\alpha}}f(\xi,\tau)dxdt\right|,\quad\Delta t>0.

Since
\omega(x,t)\in C^{\infty}(R^{N+1})
, then
\left\langle\omega(x,t)\right\rangle_{t,R^{N+1}}^{(\alpha)}=C<\infty
. And at the same time, as
it can be easily verified by scaling of the variables,
\left\langle\omega(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{t}{\varepsilon})\right\rangle_{t,R^{N+1}}^{(\alpha)}=C\varepsilon^{-\alpha}
. Besides, the support of the function

\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
is included in the set
\left\{|x|+|t|\leq\varepsilon\right\}
. Consider the following two cases.

Let first
\Delta t<\varepsilon
. Then the support of the fraction under the
integral is included in
\left\{(\xi,\tau):|\xi-x|+|t-\tau|\leq 2\varepsilon\right\}
. Consequently,

D(\Delta t)\leq\left\langle\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\right\rangle_{t,R^{N+1}}^{(\alpha)}{\int\limits_{\left\{(\xi,\tau):|\xi-x|+|t-\tau|\leq 2\varepsilon\right\}}}\left|f(\xi,\tau)\right|dxdt\leq

\leq C\varepsilon^{-\alpha-N-1}\left\|f\right\|_{L_{\infty}(R^{N+1})}{\int\limits_{\left\{(\xi,\tau):|\xi-x|+|t-\tau|\leq 2\varepsilon\right\}}}dxdt=C\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\left\|f\right\|_{L_{\infty}(R^{N+1})}.

If now
\Delta t\geq\varepsilon
, then

D(\Delta t)\leq\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\left(\left|f_{\varepsilon}(x,t+\Delta t)\right|+\left|f_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|\right)\leq 2\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\left\|f\right\|_{L_{\infty}(R^{N+1})},

which follows from properties of
\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
in (
9.17
).

This completes the proof.

Eventually, similar to section
9.3
, instead of problem (
10.1
),
(
10.2
) consider now the follows sequence of problems. It is required to
find such a function
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(R^{N+1})
that satisfies in the whole
R^{N+1}
equation (
10.1
) with

f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
instead of
f(x,t)
(
f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
is
defined in (
10.13
)),

Lu(x,t)\equiv u_{t}(x,t)+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u(x,t)=f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t),\quad(x,t)\in R^{N+1},

(10.20)

and the condition

u(x,t)\equiv 0,\quad t<-2\varepsilon.

(10.21)

10.3
Solvability and estimates for problem (
10.20
), (
10.21
).

By repeating verbatim the reasonings of section
9.5
(with the
application of the Fourier transform in
x
and
t
to obtain a solution to
problem (
9.15
), (
9.16
) ) we obtain a solution
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\in C^{\infty}(R^{N+1})
for equation (
10.20
) and for problem
(
10.20
), (
10.21
) in general. In terms of it’s Fourier-image

\widehat{u}_{m,\varepsilon}(\xi,\xi_{0})
, this solution has, similar to
(
9.34
), the representation

\widehat{u}_{m,\varepsilon}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\frac{\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})}{i\xi_{0}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}.

(10.22)

Besides, similar to obtaining property (
9.33
) in section
9.5
, we
have for
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
property (
10.21
) that is (in view of

u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\in C^{\infty}(R^{N+1})
)

u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\equiv 0,\quad t\leq-2\varepsilon.

(10.23)

And for the derivatives
\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)/\partial t
and

(-\Delta_{z_{i}})^{\frac{\sigma_{i}}{2}}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
we have the
representations in terms of the Fourier images

\frac{\widehat{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}}}{\partial t}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\frac{i\xi_{0}}{i\xi_{0}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})\equiv\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0}),

(10.24)

\widehat{(-\Delta_{z_{i}})^{\frac{\sigma_{i}}{2}}u_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\frac{|\zeta_{i}|^{\sigma_{i}}}{i\xi_{0}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})\equiv\widehat{m}_{i}(\xi,\xi_{0})\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0}).

(10.25)

Replicating the reasoning of section
9.6
with the application of
Theorem
8.1
to obtain estimate (
9.44
), we obtain from
representation (
10.24
) on the ground of this theorem the estimate for the
Hölder seminorm of the derivative of
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
in
t
in
the whole space
R^{N+1}

\left\langle\frac{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t}\right\rangle_{t,R^{N+1}}^{(\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma})\left\langle f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right\rangle_{R^{N+1}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\varepsilon^{-\alpha}|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)},

(10.26)

where the second inequality follows from (
10.19
). This estimates contains
in it’s right hand side the singular factor
\varepsilon^{-\alpha}
and this
estimate is temporary and technical. The similar estimate is valid also for
the highest Hölder seminorms in
x
- variables, but for now estimate
(
10.26
) is sufficient for us.

The main step to obtain the estimate for the highest Hölder seminorm of
the solution
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
is an application of Theorem
8.2

to representation (
10.25
). The direct verification shows that for the
functions
f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
and for the multipliers
\widehat{m}_{i}(\xi,\xi_{0})
in (
10.25
) all the conditions of Theorem
8.2

are satisfied if
\alpha
is sufficiently small. Firstly, functions

f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
are finite in
R^{N+1}
and consequently belong to

L_{2}(R^{N+1})
. Besides, in view of (
10.18
), these functions have
uniformly bounded in
m
and
\varepsilon
Hölder seminorms in the space
variables
x
. Further, the multiplier
\widehat{m}_{i}(\xi,\xi_{0})
in
(
10.25
) possesses properties (
8.17
), (
8.18
) if one takes

\xi^{(1)}\equiv\xi
,
\xi^{(2)}\equiv\xi_{0}
. Finally, exactly as it was in
section
9.6
, by replicating relations (
9.37
) - (
9.43
),
one can verify conditions (
8.19
), (
8.20
) of Theorem
8.2
if

\alpha\in(0,1)
is sufficiently small. Similar to (
9.37
) -
(
9.43
) we choose
s_{0}=1
,
s_{k}=N_{k}
. At that, in the present case
of the application of Theorem
8.2
, under a choice of
\delta
from
condition (
9.43
), conditions (
9.39
), (
9.40
) acquire a
more rigid form

1=s_{0}>\frac{N_{0}}{p}+\alpha=\frac{1}{p}+\alpha=1-\delta+\alpha,

s_{k}=N_{k}>\frac{N_{k}}{p}+\alpha=N_{k}(1-\delta)+\alpha.

But these conditions are evidently satisfied if
\alpha<\delta
, where

\delta
is chosen from condition (
9.43
). Consequently, on the ground of
Theorem
8.2
and (
10.18
) we have the estimate for solutions

u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
to (
10.20
), (
10.21
)

{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{r}}\left\langle\left(-\Delta_{z_{k}}\right)^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u_{m,\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{R^{N+1}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\{N_{k}\})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)},\quad m=1,2,...,\varepsilon\in(0,1).

(10.27)

And from this, on the ground of (
3.11
), it follows, in particular, that

u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
has the desired smoothness in
x

\left\langle u_{m,\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{x,R^{N+1}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha)}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\{N_{k}\})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)},\quad m=1,2,...,\varepsilon\in(0,1).

(10.28)

As for the highest Hölder seminorm in
t
that is
\left\langle u_{m,\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(1+\alpha)}=\left\langle\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)/\partial t\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\alpha)}
, it’s estimate over the set

\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
follows directly from equation (
10.20
) (which is
satisfied in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
by the functions
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
)
and from (
10.27
), (
10.15
)

\left\langle\frac{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\alpha)}\leq{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{r}}\left\langle\left(-\Delta_{z_{k}}\right)^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u_{m,\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\alpha)}+\left\langle f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}.

(10.29)

Thus estimates (
10.28
) and (
10.29
) give the full estimate of the
highest Hölder seminorm over
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
that is

\left\langle u_{m,\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,1+\alpha)}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\{N_{k}\})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)},\quad m=1,2,...,\varepsilon\in(0,1).

(10.30)

To finalize the estimate we need the behavior of the functions

u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
and
\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)/\partial t
at

t=0
with respect to
m
and
\varepsilon
, and also we need the estimates of
the module maxima for functions
\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(0)}
. Firstly, from (
10.23
) ((
10.21
)) and
(
10.19
) it follows that for each
t\in[-2\varepsilon,0]
we have

\left|\frac{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t}\right|=\left|\frac{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t}-\frac{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,-2\varepsilon)}{\partial t}\right|\leq

\leq\left\langle u_{m,\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{t,R^{N+1}}^{(1+\alpha)}|-2\varepsilon|^{\alpha}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)},\quad t\in[-2\varepsilon,0].

(10.31)

Consequently,

\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,0)\right|\leq{\int\limits_{-2\varepsilon}^{0}}\left|\frac{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t}\right|dt\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}\varepsilon.

(10.32)

Now from (
10.29
) and (
10.31
) we infer the estimate for
\left|\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)/\partial t\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(0)}
,

\left|\frac{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t}\right|\leq\left|\frac{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,0)}{\partial t}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(0)}+\left\langle\frac{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\alpha)}t^{\alpha}\leq

\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}\left(1+T^{\alpha}\right).

And this permits to obtain, at last, the estimate for
\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(0)}
for

t\in[0,T]

\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|\leq\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,0)\right|+{\int\limits_{0}^{t}}\left|\frac{\partial u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,\tau)}{\partial\tau}\right|d\tau\leq

\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}(\varepsilon+t\left(1+T^{\alpha}\right)),

that is

\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}(\varepsilon+T(\left(1+T^{\alpha}\right))).

(10.33)

Thus, combining the reasoning and the estimates of this section, we have
proved the following assertion.

Lemma 10.4

There exists such
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\overline{\sigma},\{N_{k}\})
that for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{0}]
problems (
10.20
),
(
10.21
) have solutions
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(R^{N+1})
with the uniformly bounded in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
norms

\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,1+\alpha)}\leq C(T,\overline{\sigma})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)},

(10.34)

and

\left\langle u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,1+\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}

(10.35)

with some constant
C(\theta,\overline{\sigma})
, which does not depend on
T
.

Moreover, for an arbitrary
\widetilde{T}\leq T

\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}(1+\widetilde{T}^{1+\alpha}),

(10.36)

and also estimate (
10.32
) is valid at
t=0
.

10.4
Solvability and estimates of the solutions to problem (
10.1
),
(
10.2
). Extension of the solution and it’s uniqueness.

Exactly as it was done in section
9.7
under the the proof of
Proposition
9.2
, we can go to the limit at
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
,

m\rightarrow\infty
in the sequence of problems (
10.20
), (
10.21
).
Similar to section
9.7
, this is grounded on the convergence on compact
sets
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
of the sequence of the mollified functions

f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
to the original function
f(x,t)
in the sense of
(
10.16
). Besides, due to the estimates of Lemma
10.4
, a subsequence
of the solutions
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
has the analogous convergence,
completely similar to (
9.57
) - (
9.59
). The only difference from
section
9.7
is that the sequence of the solutions
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
does not vanish at
t=0
automatically. However, estimate (
10.32
)
at
t=0
tells that the limiting function
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
not only satisfies equation
(
10.1
), but vanishes at
t=0
according to the zero initial condition.
Therefore, exactly replicating reasonings of section
9.7
, including the
proof of Corollary
9.3
and the proof of Proposition
9.5
of
section
9.8
on the extension of the solution, we arrive at the
following assertion.

Proposition 10.5

There exists such
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\overline{\sigma},\{N_{k}\})
that for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{0}]
and under conditions
(
10.3
), (
10.4
) problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) has a solution

u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})

with estimates (
10.5
) - (
10.7
).

In the case when
f(x,t)
is defined for all
t>0
in the domain

\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}=R^{N}\times[0,\infty)
and it’s norm in the
space
C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
,

\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{0}]
, is finite that is
\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}<\infty

problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) has such a solution
u(x,t)
that locally in
time
t
belongs to the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
and that obeys estimates (
10.6
),
(
10.7
) that is

\left\langle u\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

(10.37)

\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right)(1+\widetilde{T}^{1+\alpha})+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(0)},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq\infty.

(10.38)

Consequently, for each finite
T>0
estimate (
10.5
) is also valid.

Besides, let a solution
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
,
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{0}]
, to problem (
10.1
),
(
10.2
) in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
be fixed and let the right hand side

f(x,t)
in (
10.1
) be defined (or extended from
[0,T]
) for all
t>0
on
the domain
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}
, and
f(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha}(\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}})
. Then there exists such solution
to problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) in
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}
(denoted by
the same symbol
u(x,t)
) that belongs to
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}})
for each
\widetilde{T}>0
and
that coincides with
u(x,t)
in
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
with the analogous to
(
10.37
), (
10.38
) estimates in
\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}

\left\langle u\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,1+\alpha)}\right),

(10.39)

\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,1+\alpha)}\right)(1+\widetilde{T}^{1+\alpha})+\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,1+\alpha)},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq\infty.

(10.40)

Note that the proof of the last assertion of this proposition about the
extension of the solution is not only a replication of the proof to
Proposition
9.5
but is the significant simplification of the last. In
particular, due to locality of the first derivative in
t
, we do not need an
additional term in the right hand side of the equation as it was in equation
(
9.74
), and consequently, we do not need an additional lemma of the kind
of Lemma
9.4
. Besides, we do not need to reduce the smoothness exponent
from
\alpha
to
\beta<\alpha
to achieve the agreement of the solution at

t=T
as it was done in Proposition
9.5
. This reflect the known
semigroup property of Cauchy problems with the first time derivative.

On the ground of Proposition
10.5
on the extension of the solution and
by the simple replication of the proof of Lemma
9.6
we get the
assertion about the uniqueness of the solution from
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
to problem (
10.1
),
(
10.2
). We stress that in this place we do not need any restriction on
the smoothness exponent
\alpha
to have it sufficiently small since the space
with a bigger
\alpha
is continuously embedded in each space with a less

\alpha
.

Proposition 10.6

Problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) has at most one solution from
the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
.

10.5
Removing the restriction on the Hölder exponent
0<\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\overline{\sigma},\{N_{k}\})
and completion of the
proof of Theorem
10.1
.

Let now in problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) the exponent
\alpha
satisfies
instead of conditions (
10.4
) the only condition

\alpha\in(0,1),

(10.41)

without the restriction
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}\in(0,1)
. Moreover, since the
case of a sufficiently small
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
is already considered
above, we assume, to be specific, that
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},1)
. Since the
functions
f(x,t)
and
u_{0}(x)
satisfy condition (
10.3
), then they
satisfy the more weak condition

f(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha_{0},\alpha_{0}}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}}),\quad u_{0}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha_{0})}(R^{N}).

This follows from the continuous embeddings
C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})\subset C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha_{0},\alpha_{0}}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
and
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)}(R^{N})\subset C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha_{0})}(R^{N})
in view of the
assumption
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},1)
. On the ground of Propositions
10.5

and
10.6
there exists the unique solution
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha_{0}),1+\alpha_{0}}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
to problem
(
10.1
), (
10.2
) with estimates (
10.5
) - (
10.7
) with the
exponent
\alpha_{0}
instead of
\alpha
. We show below that in fact the
solution belongs to the class
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
and estimates (
10.5
) - (
10.7
)
are valid with the bigger exponent
\alpha
itself.

Let an index
k\in\{1,...,r\}
be fixed and let a non-zero
h\in R^{N_{k}}
be
also fixed, where
R^{N_{k}}
is the corresponding subspace of
R^{N}
,
containing space variables
z_{k}
. For
\omega\in(0,1)
and
m>\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha
consider the function

u_{k}(x,t)\equiv\frac{\delta_{h,z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)}{|h|^{\rho}},\quad\rho\equiv(1-\omega)\sigma_{k}\alpha,\quad\omega\in(0,1),\quad\alpha_{k}\equiv\omega\alpha,

(10.42)

where
\omega
is chosen so small that
\alpha_{k}\equiv\omega\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
, and such that
\rho
,
\alpha_{k}
,
\sigma_{i}\alpha_{k}
, and

\sigma_{i}+\sigma_{i}\alpha_{k}
are noninteger and
\sigma_{i}\alpha_{k}\in(0,1)
,
i=1,2,...,r
. In view of linearity of problem (
10.1
),
(
10.2
), it can be directly verified that the function
u_{k}(x,t)

satisfies this problem with the following right hand side and the initial data
correspondingly

f_{k}(x,t)\equiv\frac{\delta_{h,z_{k}}^{m}f(x,t)}{|h|^{\rho}},\quad u_{0,k}(x)\equiv\frac{\delta_{h,z_{k}}^{m}u_{0}(x)}{|h|^{\rho}}.

(10.43)

According to Proposition
6.7
, the functions
f_{k}(x,t)
and

u_{0,k}(x)
belong to the spaces

f_{k}(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha_{k},\alpha_{k}}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}}),\quad u_{0,k}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha_{k})}(R^{N}),

and

\left|f_{k}\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha_{k},\alpha_{k})}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega)\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)},\quad\left|u_{0,k}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha_{k}))}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega)\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}.

(10.44)

From Propositions
10.5
and
10.6
and from (
10.44
) it follows
that
u_{k}(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha_{k}),1+\alpha_{k}}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
, and

\left\langle u_{k}\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha_{k}),1+\alpha_{k})}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha_{k})\left(\left|f_{k}\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha_{k},\alpha_{k})}+\left|u_{0,k}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha_{k}))}\right)\leq

\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega)\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.45)

In particular, this estimate includes the highest Hölder seminorm with
respect to the group of the space variables
z_{k}

\left\langle u_{k}\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha_{k})}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega)\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right)

that is in view of property (
2.4
) (remind that
m>\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha
),

\sup_{s\in R^{N_{k}},s\neq 0}\frac{|\delta_{s,z_{k}}^{m}u_{k}(x,t)|}{|s|^{\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha_{k}}}=C\left\langle u_{k}\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha_{k})}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega)\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.46)

Thus, according to the definition of
u_{k}(x,t)
in (
10.42
),

\sup_{s\in R^{N_{k}},s\neq 0}\frac{|\delta_{s,z_{k}}^{m}\delta_{h,z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)|}{|s|^{\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha_{k}}|h|^{\rho}}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega)\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.47)

Choosing here the particular value
s=h
and making use of the definitions of

\alpha_{k}
and
\rho
in (
10.42
), we conclude that

\frac{|\delta_{h,z_{k}}^{2m}u(x,t)|}{|h|^{\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha}}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega)\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.48)

Since
h\in R^{N_{k}}\backslash\{0\}
is arbitrary, we can infer from this, on
the ground of (
2.4
), the estimate for the highest seminorm in
z_{k}
of
the solution
u(x,t)
itself with the original
\alpha

\left\langle u(x,t)\right\rangle_{z_{k},\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha)}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega)\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.49)

It is clear that these reasonings do not depend on the index
k
of a group of
the space variables, and thus (
10.49
) is valid for each
k=1,...,r
that
is

\left\langle u(x,t)\right\rangle_{x,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega)\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.50)

On the ground of the properties of the fractional operators
(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}
from equation (
10.1
), that are formulated in
Proposition
6.1
in (
6.1
), we conclude that the consecuence of
(
10.50
) is the estimate

\left\langle{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u(x,t)\right\rangle_{x,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha)}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega,\{N_{k}\})\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.51)

But then from equation (
10.1
) and from (
10.3
) we obtain the estimate
for the Hölder seminorm of the time derivative
u_{t}(x,t)
with respect
to
x

\left\langle u_{t}(x,t)\right\rangle_{x,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha)}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega,\{N_{k}\})\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.52)

Consider again some fixed group
z_{k}
of the space variables. Based on
(
10.49
) and (
10.52
), we can apply Theorem
7.3
and it’s
estimate (
7.22
) to the function
u(x,t)
to obtain

\left\langle(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u(x,t)\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\alpha)}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega,\{N_{k}\})\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.53)

Since the group
z_{k}
is arbitrary, we can infer from the last inequality
and again from equation (
10.1
) that

\left\langle u_{t}(x,t)\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\alpha)}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\omega,\{N_{k}\})\left(\left|f\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+\left|u_{0}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right).

(10.54)

Combining now estimates (
10.50
), (
10.54
) (and estimate (
10.5
)
for the solution
u(x,t)
in the weaker space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha_{0}),1+\alpha_{0}}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
, containing the estimate
of the lowest norm
\left|u(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(0)}
), we get finally

|u|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,T)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

which completes the proof of Theorem
10.1
.

11
Problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) for an arbitrary
\alpha>0
.

In the previous section we’ve considered problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
)
under the restriction
\alpha\in(0,1)
. This imposes the restriction on the
smoothness of the solution in
t
within one integer derivative in
t
with
the finite Hölder seminorm in
t
of order
\alpha\in(0,1)
. This also
imposes the restriction on the smoothness of the solution in
x
to the
anisotropic orders
\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha
. In this section, we will
get rid of the restriction
\alpha\in(0,1)
, which will allow to consider
problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) in anisotropic Hölder spaces of arbitrary smoothness.

So, let first in problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) we have
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
let (
10.3
) be satisfied. Then, on the ground of Theorem
10.1
, this
problem has the unique solution from the space
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),1+\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
,
\beta\equiv\alpha-1\in(0,1)
, due
to the fact that the right hand side and the initial data are even surplus smooth.

Show first the additional smoothness of the solution in
t
. For this we will
simply differentiate equation (
10.1
) in
t
to reduce the problem with a
bigger
\alpha
to a problem with a smaller one. Formally differentiating
equation (
10.1
) in
t
and denoting (formally for a while) the derivative

u_{t}(x,t)
by
v(x,t)\equiv u_{t}(x,t)
, we obtain for
v(x,t)
the same
equation, but with the another right hand side

Lu(x,t)\equiv v_{t}(x,t)+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}v=g(x,t)\equiv f_{t}(x,t),\quad(x,t)\in R_{T}^{N}.

(11.1)

The initial condition for the function
v(x,t)\equiv u_{t}(x,t)
at
t=0
is
defined from the original equation (
10.1
), which shows that at
t=0

v(x,0)=v_{0}(x)\equiv f(x,0)-{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}u_{0}(x),\quad x\in R^{N}.

(11.2)

Making use of formulas (
2.5
), (
2.6
) and Theorem
6.4
to
calculate the spaces of smoothness for the functions
g(x,t)
and
v_{0}(x)
,
from (
11.1
) and (
11.2
), with taking the notation

\beta\equiv\alpha-1\in(0,1),

(11.3)

we obtain that

g(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\beta,\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}}),\quad v_{0}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta)}(R^{N})

(11.4)

and the following estimates are valid

|g|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\beta)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,T)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)},\quad|v_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta))}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}.

(11.5)

Based on Theorem
10.1
, we infer that problem (
11.1
), (
11.2
)
has the unique solution
v(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),1+\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
and

|v|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),1+\beta)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,T)\left(|g|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\beta)}+|v_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta))}\right).

(11.6)

The direct verification with taking into account the definitions of
g(x,t)

in (
11.1
) and of
v_{0}(x)
in (
11.2
) shows that the function

\widetilde{u}(x,t)\equiv u_{0}(x)+{\int\limits_{0}^{t}}v(x,\tau)d\tau

satisfies the original problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
). Since in addition

\widetilde{u}(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),1+\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
on the ground of (
11.6
), then from the uniqueness in this
class it follows that
\widetilde{u}(x,t)
coincides with the original
solution
u(x,t)
that is, in particular,
v(x,t)\equiv u_{t}(x,t)
not
formally but in fact. But then
u_{t}(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),1+\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
and for this function estimate
(
11.6
) is valid. This means, in particular, that

\left\langle u_{t}(x,t)\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(1+\beta)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,T)\left(|g|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\beta)}+|v_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta))}\right),

and from this, on the ground of the definition of
\beta
, of the definition
of Hölder seminorms, and of (
11.5
), we obtain the higher smoothness
of the solution
u(x,t)
in
t
that is

\left\langle u(x,t)\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(1+\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,T)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),\quad\alpha\in(0,2).

(11.7)

Turning now to the additional smoothness in
x
, we confine ourselves to a
brief explanation because the proof is identical to that from section

10.5
at the removing of the restriction on the smallness of
\alpha
. At
that we consider the functions (
h\in R^{N_{k}}
)

u_{k}(x,t)\equiv\frac{\delta_{h,z_{k}}^{m}u(x,t)}{|h|^{\rho}},\quad\rho\equiv(1-\omega)\sigma_{k}\alpha,\quad\omega\in(0,1),\quad\alpha_{k}\equiv\omega\alpha,

where similar to the previous reasonings
\omega
is chosen such that

\alpha_{k}\equiv\omega\alpha\in(0,1)
and also such that the numbers
\rho
,

\alpha_{k}
,
\sigma_{i}\alpha_{k}
, and
\sigma_{i}+\sigma_{i}\alpha_{k}

are nonintegers. Further reasonings also coincide with reasonings from section

10.5
, which gives

\left\langle u(x,t)\right\rangle_{x,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{\left(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)\right)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,T)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),\quad\alpha\in(0,2).

(11.8)

Estimates (
11.7
) and (
11.8
) mean that Theorem
10.1
is valid
not only for
\alpha\in(0,1)
but
\alpha\in(1,2)
as well that is for a
noninteger
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Iterating this process by induction, we, finally,
arrive at the following assertion.

Theorem 11.1

If
\alpha>0
is a noninteger and conditions (
10.3
) are
satisfied, then problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) has the unique solution

u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})

with the estimates

|u|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,T)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

(11.9)

\left\langle u\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),1+\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

(11.10)

\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right)\widetilde{T}^{1+\alpha}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(0)},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq T.

(11.11)

Note that estimate (
11.11
) is obtained completely similar to (
10.7
).

12
Smoothness of solution to (
9.1
), (
9.2
) for more smooth
initial data.

It was shown in the previous section that in problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
),
which contains the integer derivative in
t
of the first order, the
smoothness of the solution can be arbitrary high, depending on the smoothness
of the data. In the present section We will extend this property to solutions
of problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
). However, as we will show below, the
complete extension of this property to the case of a fractional derivative in

t
of order
\theta
requires from the right hand side of the equation to
obey a series of conditions. At that there are no restrictions for the
increasing of the smoothness in the space variables
x
and such smoothness
increases together with the increasing of the corresponding smoothness of the
data. Pretty different situation takes place with the smoothness in
t
. We
explain this with the following simple example, which completely reflects the
essence of the situation.

Example 12.1

Let in problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) the right hand side and
the initial data be equal correspondingly

f(x,t)\equiv t,\quad u_{0}(x)\equiv 0.

(12.1)

It can be directly verified that the unique (in view of Theorem
9.1
)
solution to (
9.1
), (
9.2
) with such data is the function with the
dependence on
t
solely

u(x,t)=C(\theta)t^{1+\theta}.

(12.2)

Evidently that the function
f(x,t)
belongs to any space
C^{\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with an arbitrary large

\alpha
that is, in fact, to
C^{\infty}
. However, for
\theta<1
the
solution
u(x,t)
has the smoothness in
t
in the closed domain
\{t\geq 0\}

only up to the order
1+\theta
, though this solution is infinitely smooth in

x
. In terms of the previous section about the raising of smoothness by
differentiation this corresponds to the following. After the differentiation
of equation (
9.1
) in
t
and at the consideration of the obtained from
(
9.1
) equation for
u_{t}(x,t)
we obtain the right hand side

f_{t}(x,t)\equiv 1
and the initial data
u_{t}(x,0)\equiv 0
(in view of
Proposition
3.2
). And thus the necessary condition (
9.6
) is
not satisfied. To satisfy this condition we must have in our case

f_{t}(x,0)\equiv 0
. Consequently, the violation of condition (
9.6
) is a
blockage for further raising of the smoothness in
t
in the closed domain

\{t\geq 0\}
. Thus for the further raising of the smoothness in
t
in closed
domain we must impose additional restrictions on the derivatives
f_{t}^{(k)}(x,0)
. At the same time for problem (
10.1
), (
10.2
) with an
integer derivative in
t
additional restrictions of kind (
9.6
) are not necessary.

This example shows also to some extent that for additional smoothness in
x

no additional restrictions are required.

In particular, Theorem
9.1
can be generalized as follows.

Proposition 12.2

Theorem
9.1
stays valid if condition (
9.5
) is
replaced by the more weak condition

\theta,\theta\alpha\in(0,1)

(12.3)

that is the restrictions
\sigma_{k}\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\quad k=1,...,r
, can be omitted.

We do not present here a detailed proof of this proposition since the proof
verbatim coincides with the reasonings of section
10.1
at the removing
the analogous restrictions on
\alpha
in the proof of Theorem
10.1
. The
presence in the equation either integer or fractional derivative in
t
does
not matter in these reasonings, therefore we refer the reader to section

10.1
.

Formulate now the main assertion of the present section.

Theorem 12.3

Let in problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) conditions (
9.3
),
(
9.4
), and (
9.6
) be satisfied. Let, further,
\alpha
be such that
numbers
\theta\alpha
,
\theta+\theta\alpha
,
\sigma_{k}\alpha
,
\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha
,
k=1,...,r
are nonintegers and for some positive
integer
n

\theta\alpha\in(n-1,n).

(12.4)

Let, finally, for
n\geq 2
besides the agreement condition (
9.6
) the
following condition be satisfied

\frac{\partial^{m}f(x,0)}{\partial t^{m}}\equiv 0,\quad m=1,...,n-1.

(12.5)

Then problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) has the unique solution
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})

with the estimates

|u|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha,T)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

(12.6)

\left\langle u\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right),

(12.7)

\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha))}\right)\widetilde{T}^{\theta+\theta\alpha}+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(0)},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq T.

(12.8)

Proof.
We will carry out the proof by induction in the value of
\theta\alpha
by
formal differentiation of equation (
9.1
) in
t
and reducing a problem
with more smooth data to a similar problem with less smooth data, according to
the schema of the previous section.

Thus, since for
\theta\alpha\in(0,1)
the situation is described in Theorem

9.1
and Proposition
12.2
, we suppose now that
\theta\alpha\in(1,2)
. Without loss of generality we can assume zero initial data

u_{0}(x)
in problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
), as it was explained in section

9.1
. Formally differentiating equation (
9.1
) in
t
, we obtain
the following equation for the function
v(x,t)\equiv u_{t}(x,t)

Lv(x,t)\equiv D_{\ast t}^{\theta}v(x,t)+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2}}v(x,t)=f_{t}(x,t),\quad(x,t)\in R_{T}^{N}

(12.9)

with the initial condition

v(x,0)\equiv 0,\quad x\in R^{N}.

(12.10)

Initial condition (
12.10
) is due to the fact that under the condition

u_{0}(x)\equiv 0
and for
\theta+\theta\alpha>1
(as it is in our case) we
have
u_{t}(x,0)\equiv 0
in view of (
3.6
). Note also that
differentiating in
t
the nonlocal derivative
D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)
in
equation (
9.1
), due to the fact that
u_{t}(x,0)\equiv 0
and according
to the definition of the Caputo - Jrbashyan derivative, we have

\left(D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)\right)_{t}=D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u_{t}(x,t).

(12.11)

On the ground of (
2.6
) the right hand side in (
12.9
) belongs to the
space

f_{t}(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}}),\quad\beta\equiv\frac{\theta\alpha-1}{\theta},

(12.12)

and

\left|f_{t}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\theta,\alpha)\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}.

(12.13)

As it was shown in Example
12.1
, for problem (
12.9
), (
12.10
) to
be solvable in the class
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta}

condition (
9.6
) must be met, which in our case has the form

f_{t}(x,0)\equiv 0,\quad x\in R^{N}.

(12.14)

Since according to the assumptions of the the theorem condition (
12.14
) is
met and
\theta\beta=\theta\alpha-1\in(0,1)
, then based on Theorem
9.1

and on Proposition
12.2
we infer that there exists a solution
v(x,t)

to problem (
12.9
), (
12.10
) from the class
v(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
, and

\left|v(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta)}\leq C\left|f_{t}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\beta,\theta\beta)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,\theta,T,N)\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}.

(12.15)

Consider the function

u(x,t)\equiv{\int\limits_{0}^{t}}v(x,\tau)d\tau.

(12.16)

From (
12.11
) it follows that

D_{\ast t}^{\theta}u(x,t)={\int\limits_{0}^{t}}D_{\ast\tau}^{\theta}v(x,\tau)d\tau.

Now from the last relation and from (
12.14
) it follows that the function

u(x,t)
satisfies problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) with zero initial
condition. Besides, on the ground of (
12.16
) and (
12.15
) we infer
that
u(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\beta),\theta+\theta\beta}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
and, consequently, it is the unique solution to
problem (
9.1
), (
9.2
) from the pointed above or more smooth class.
At the same time, again from (
12.16
) and (
12.15
) it follows that

u(x,t)
has the smoothness in
t
up to the order
\theta+\theta\beta+1=\theta+\theta\alpha
and

\left\langle u(x,t)\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,\theta,T,N)\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\theta\alpha)}.

(12.17)

As for the smoothness in
x
up to the orders
\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha

(in each group of space variables
z_{k}
), it is proved verbatim to the
corresponding reasonings from section
10.1
, which proves Theorem

12.3
for the case
\theta\alpha\in(0,2)
.

Finally, the general case
\theta\alpha\in(n-1,n)
with an arbitrary positive
integer
n
is obtained by induction in
n
, which completes the proof of the theorem.

13
Construction of functions from
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
from their initial data at

t=0
.

In the present section we describe a way of constructing a function
w(x,t)

from the class
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with
\theta+\theta\alpha>1
according to it’s initial
data at
t=0
. These initial data are the trace at
t=0
of a function

w(x,0)
itself and the traces of it’s derivatives in
t
up to the order

[\theta]
regardless of whether
\theta
is integer or fractional. The
constructing of such function is an important technical device in
investigations of parabolic initial value problems including (
1.2
),
(
1.3
). This permits to reduce investigations of a problem to it’s
investigations in the case of zero initial data and to consider the problem in
the classes of such functions that vanish at
t=0
together with all their
possible derivatives in
t
. This approach is rather standard nowdays at
different considerations of parabolic problems and was applied, in particular,
in the classical paper
[
35
]
. In section
9
it was applied for the
extension of a solution to the domain
t<0
with the class preservation, which
permitted to consider the problem in the whole space
R^{N+1}
. In section

10
we did not have such opportunity yet and the extension to the domain

t<0
was performed without class preservation in
t
, which caused some more
complex structure of the proof. If now we are going to consider the problem of
higher order with integer or fractional
\theta>1
, then the extension of a
solution to the domain
t<0
without class preservation with the aim of the
considerations in the whole
R^{N+1}
would lead to emergence in the equation
of supported at
t=0
delta-functions. Clearly, such way potentially could
make the investigations even more complicated.

Naturally, the mentioned function
w(x,t)
is not unique and the way of it’s
constructing, we present below, closely reproduces the construction from

[
37
]
, Ch. IV, Theorem 4.3. The only difference consists of the
making use of a parabolic operator with some fractional Laplace operator
instead of the standard heat operator. However, the author have not managed to
produce an algorithm to construct such
w(x,t)
for an arbitrary value of

\alpha
. The problem with following
[
37
]
is that we must have some
results on the solvability and the estimates for the simplest Cauchy problems
with the initial data from the less smooth spaces than the order of the
corresponding differential operator. For the operator of the heat equation
(and much more general - see
[
35
]
) such results are known due to
estimates of the corresponding parabolic potentials, unlike the situation with
fractional operators. Therefore in the present section we first confine
ourselves to the case of somewhat heightened exponent
\alpha
. Namely, we
suppose that

\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha>1.

(13.1)

Before we turn to the strict statements of this section, we describe, on the
ground of property (
2.6
) of anisotropic Hölder spaces, the
particular functional classes for the derivatives in
t
up to the order

[\theta]
of a function
w(x,t)
from the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
. And also we formulate
some definitions.

Let
w(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with
\theta>1
so that
[\theta]\geq 1
, and
consequently the function
w(x,t)
has the first derivative in
t
. According
to (
2.6
) the derivative
w_{t}(x,t)
belongs to the space

w_{t}(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)\frac{\theta+\theta\alpha-1}{\theta+\theta\alpha},\theta+\theta\alpha-1}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})=C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{1}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-1}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}}).

(13.2)

That is after the differentiation in
t
the smoothness in
t
decreases by
one, and the smoothness in a group of the space variables
z_{k}
decreases
by
\sigma_{k}/\theta
. Somewhat transforming the smoothness exponents of the
space in (
13.2
), we formulate (
13.2
) as follows

w_{t}(x,t)\in C^{\frac{\overline{\sigma}}{\theta}(1+\alpha(\theta,1)),1+\alpha(\theta,1)}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}}),

(13.3)

where

\frac{\overline{\sigma}}{\theta}\equiv\{\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\theta},...,\frac{\sigma_{r}}{\theta}\},\quad\alpha(\theta,1)\equiv\theta-1+\theta\alpha-1>0,

(13.4)

and we note that positivity of the exponent
\alpha(\theta,1)
follows from
assumption (
13.1
). Generally, if
[\theta]=n\geq 1
, then proceeding with
the differentiating in
t
as it is described in (
13.2
) - (
13.4
),
we get by induction

\frac{\partial^{i}w(x,t)}{\partial t^{i}}\in C^{\frac{\overline{\sigma}}{\theta}(1+\alpha(\theta,i)),1+\alpha(\theta,i)}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})=C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}}),\quad i=1,2,...n,

(13.5)

where

\alpha(\theta,i)\equiv\theta-i+\theta\alpha-1>0

(13.6)

and

\left|\frac{\partial^{i}w(x,t)}{\partial t^{i}}\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i)}\leq C\left|w(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}.

(13.7)

Besides, applying relation (
2.6
), we infer that the differentiating in

t
is a bounded linear operator from
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})

with
i\leq n-1
to
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i+1}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i-1}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
that is

\left|\frac{\partial^{i+1}w(x,t)}{\partial t^{i+1}}\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i+1}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i-1)}\leq C\left|\frac{\partial^{i}w(x,t)}{\partial t^{i}}\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i)}.

(13.8)

We note in addition the following. If the exponents
\theta\alpha
and

\sigma_{k}\alpha
,
k=1,...,r
, (the ”additional” smoothness exponents) are
nonintegers, then for each
