i=1,2,...n
the ”additional” smoothness exponents

(\theta-i)\alpha(\theta,i)=\theta\alpha
and
\sigma_{k}(\theta,i)\alpha(\theta,i)=\sigma_{k}\alpha
stay in fact the same nonintegers.

We need also the fact that in view of Theorem
6.4
with
\overline{\rho}=\overline{\sigma}/\theta
the operators
(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2\theta}}
,
k=1,...,r
, are, similar to the
differentiating in
t
, linear bounded operators from
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
to
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{1}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-1}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
. That is for
w(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})

\left|(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2\theta}}w(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{1}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-1)}\leq C\left|w(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}.

(13.9)

Besides, based on Theorem
6.4
we infer that similar to (
13.7
),
(
13.8
) we have for iterations of the operators
(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2\theta}}

\left|\left[(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2\theta}}\right]^{i}w(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i)}\leq C\left|w(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)},i\leq n.

(13.10)

And moreover for
i\leq n-1

\left|(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2\theta}}\left\{\left[(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2\theta}}\right]^{i}w(x,t)\right\}\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i+1}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i-1)}\leq

(13.11)

\leq C\left|\left[(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2\theta}}\right]^{i}w(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i)}

that is
(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2\theta}}
is a bounded linear
operator from
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with
i\leq n-1
to

C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i+1}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i-1}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
.

Formulate now the main assertion of the present section.

Theorem 13.1

Let such positive integer or noninteger numbers
\theta
,

\sigma_{k}
,
k=1,...,r
, and
\alpha
be given that the numbers

\theta\alpha
,
\theta+\theta\alpha
,
\sigma_{k}\alpha
, and
\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha
are nonintegers. Let, further, condition (
13.1
) is
met. Let, finally, such
n+1=[\theta]+1
functions
\varphi_{i}(x)
,

i=0,...,n
, be given that they are defined in
R^{N}
and belong to the
spaces (
\overline{\sigma}\equiv\{\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{r}\}
)

\varphi_{i}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma}}(R^{N}),\quad i=0,...,n.

(13.12)

Then there exists such functions
w(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
that at
t=0

\frac{\partial^{i}w(x,0)}{\partial t^{i}}=\varphi_{i}(x),\quad i=0,...,n

(13.13)

and

\left|w(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C{\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}}\left|\varphi_{i}(x)\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma})}.

(13.14)

Proof.
The schema of proof for this theorem coincides with that from Theorem 4.3, Ch.
IV in
[
37
]
. However we present the proof here for completeness
because instead of the standard heat operator with well known properties,
which was used in
[
37
]
, we make use of the operator that was
investigated above in sections
10
,
11
.

Define the differential operator

L\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+M\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}(-\Delta_{z_{k}})^{\frac{\sigma_{k}}{2\theta}}.

(13.15)

From (
13.5
), (
13.6
), (
13.10
), and (
13.11
) it follows
that
L
is a linear bounded operator from the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with
0\leq i\leq n-1
to the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i+1}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i-1}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
,

L:C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})\rightarrow C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i+1}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i-1}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}}),\quad 0\leq i\leq n-1.

(13.16)

Define, further, the functions

\psi_{j}(x)\equiv{\sum_{s=0}^{j}}C_{j}^{s}M^{s}\varphi_{j-s}(x),\quad j=0,...,n.

(13.17)

Let a function
w(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(R_{T}^{N})
satisfies the condition

\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+M\right)^{j}w(x,t)\right|_{t=0}=\psi_{j}(x),\quad j=0,...,n,

(13.18)

where all relations are correctly defined in view of (
13.16
). Show that
then this function satisfies also conditions(
13.13
). Taking into account
the definition of the function
\psi_{j}(x)
in (
13.17
), condition
(
13.18
) can be formulated in the form

{\sum_{s=0}^{j}}C_{j}^{s}M^{s}\left[\left.\frac{\partial^{j-s}w(x,t)}{\partial t^{j-s}}\right|_{t=0}-\varphi_{j-s}(x)\right]=0,\quad j=0,...,n.

(13.19)

From this condition we get by induction starting from
j=0

w(x,t)|_{t=0}=\varphi_{0}(x).

Further, making use of this relation, we infer from (
13.19
) for
j=1

that

\left.\frac{\partial w(x,t)}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}=\varphi_{1}(x).

Proceeding this process by induction we verify all the relations in (
13.13
).

Define now the function
w(x,t)
we need recursively from the Cauchy problem

\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}+Mw=w^{(1)}(x,t),\quad w(x,0)=\psi_{0}w(x)=\varphi_{0}(x),

(13.20)

where the function
w^{(1)}(x,t)
is defined in advance from the problem

\frac{\partial w^{(1)}}{\partial t}+Mw^{(1)}=w^{(2)}(x,t),\quad w^{(1)}(x,0)=\psi_{1}(x),

(13.21)

and so on. And the initial function
w^{(n)}
is defined from the problem

\frac{\partial w^{(n)}}{\partial t}+Mw^{(n)}=0,\quad w^{(n)}(x,0)=\psi_{n}(x).

(13.22)

On the base of Theorem
11.1
all functions
w^{(j)}(x,t)
are correctly
defined, since all spaces
C^{\frac{\overline{\sigma}}{\theta}(1+\alpha(\theta,i)),1+\alpha(\theta,i)}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})=C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},\theta+\theta\alpha-i}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
with
\alpha(\theta,i)>0
in the above Cauchy problems
are appropriate for the application of Theorem
11.1
.

Since, in view of the definition,

w^{(j)}(x,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+M\right)^{j}w(x,t),

then the function
w(x,t)
satisfies conditions (
13.18
) and, consequently
(
13.13
) by the construction of
w^{(j)}(x,t)
.

Estimate (
13.14
) is obtained now by the successive application of Theorem

11.1
to the chain of problems (
13.20
) - (
13.22
).

We stress one more that requirement (
13.1
) in this theorem is due to the
fact that the least smooth initial data in problem (
13.22
) belongs to the
the space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{n}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},}(R^{N})=C^{\frac{\overline{\sigma}}{\theta}(1+\alpha(\theta,n))}(R^{N})

with

\alpha(\theta,n)=\theta-n+\theta\alpha-1=\theta-[\theta]+\theta\alpha-1=\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha-1.

Therefore
\alpha(\theta,n)
is negative under violation of condition
(
13.1
) (
\alpha(\theta,n)
can not be equal to zero since
\theta-n+\theta\alpha
is a noninteger by the assumption). At the same time the
total smoothness exponent for the space
\psi_{n}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{n}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},}(R^{N})
is positive and for
the case of integer derivatives in
[
35
]
by methods of parabolic
potentials sharp estimates for the solutions of the corresponding problems
(
13.22
) for parabolic systems were obtained. As for the fractional
problem under consideration, such estimates are unknown by now.

However, if
\theta\in(0,2)
that is
n=[\theta]\leq 1
, the the desired
function can be constructed without condition (
13.1
). For
n=0
the
construction is trivial and for
n=1
such function can be constructed as the
solution of the problem

\frac{\partial w(x,t)}{\partial t}+Mw=\varphi_{1}(x)+M\varphi_{0}(x),(x,t)\in R_{T}^{N};\quad w(x,0)=\varphi_{0}(x),x\in R^{N}.

(13.23)

Here the initial data
\varphi_{0}(x)
and the right hand side
\varphi_{1}(x)+M\varphi_{0}(x)
belong to the appropriate for the application of
Theorem
10.1
space and consequently problem (
13.23
) has the unique
solution from the desired class with the corresponding estimate of it’s norm
over the norms of the functions
\varphi_{0}(x)
and
\varphi_{1}(x)
. Thus
the following assertion is valid.

Theorem 13.2

For
\theta\in(0,2)
Theorem
13.1
stays valid without
restriction (
13.1
).

Note finally that if we consider to construct a function
w(x,t)
from the
space
C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
not by the full set of the traces of it’s integer derivatives in
t

up to the order
n=[\theta]
but by the set of the traces of it’s integer
derivatives in
t
up to the order
n-1=[\theta]-1
, then we can do without
(
13.1
). Indeed, in this case the last (the initial) problem in the chain
of problems (
13.20
) - (
13.22
) is not the problem with the number
n
,
but the one with the number
n-1

\frac{\partial w^{(n-1)}}{\partial t}+Mw^{(n-1)}=0,\quad w^{(n-1)}(x,0)=\psi_{n-1}(x),

(13.24)

where

\psi_{n-1}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{n-1}{\theta}\overline{\sigma},}(R^{N})=C^{\frac{\overline{\sigma}}{\theta}(1+\alpha(\theta,n-1))}(R^{N}),

and

\alpha(\theta,n-1)=\theta-(n-1)+\theta\alpha-1=\theta-[\theta]+\theta\alpha=\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha>0.

Thus the simple replication of the proof of Theorem
13.1
leads to the
following (more weak in a sense) assertion without restriction (
13.1
).

Theorem 13.3

Let such positive integer or noninteger numbers
\theta
,

\sigma_{k}
,
k=1,...,r
, and
\alpha
be given that the numbers

\theta\alpha
,
\theta+\theta\alpha
,
\sigma_{k}\alpha
, and
\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{k}\alpha
are nonintegers. Let also such
n=[\theta]
functions

\varphi_{i}(x)
,
i=0,...,n-1
, be given that they are defined in
R^{N}
and
belong to the spaces (
\overline{\sigma}\equiv\{\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{r}\}
)

\varphi_{i}(x)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma}}(R^{N}),\quad i=0,...,n-1.

(13.25)

Then there exists such a function
w(x,t)\in C^{\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha}(\overline{R_{T}^{N}})
that at
t=0

\frac{\partial^{i}w(x,0)}{\partial t^{i}}=\varphi_{i}(x),\quad i=0,...,n-1

(13.26)

and

\left|w(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),\theta+\theta\alpha)}\leq C{\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}}\left|\varphi_{i}(x)\right|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{\theta}\overline{\sigma})}.

(13.27)

14
The proofs of theorems
2.1
,
2.3
, and
2.5
.

In the present section we outline the proof of theorems
2.1
,

2.3
,
2.5
. But we do not present here the detailed proofs since
they, in fact, would be simple verbatim copies of the reasoning and
constructions of the proofs for the main assertions from the previous
sections. In particular, Theorem
2.1
is a direct generalization to the
case
\theta=n\geq 1
of Theorem
11.1
, and Theorem
2.3

generalizes, evidently, Theorem
12.3
to the case of an arbitrary
noninteger
\theta>0
. These theorems were not proved in their full generality
in the previous sections
9
and
10
since the preliminary
considerations of the cases
\theta\in(0,1)
and
\theta=1
were necessary for
the only reason to obtain the results of section
13
on the extension of
the initial data to the domain
\{t>0\}
. Without this extension it would not
be possible to reduce Cauchy problems of higher order in
t
to the case of
zero initial data with the subsequent extension of solutions by zero to the
domain
\{t<0\}
. After such reducing all others steps in the corresponding
proofs stays unaltered.

As for Theorem
2.5
, it is a direct corollary of Proposition
6.3
,
Proposition
6.8
, and Theorem
9.1
.

Therefore we comment only on the proofs of Theorems
2.1
and
2.3
.

14.1
Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

For the case
n=1
Theorem
2.1
coincides with Theorem
11.1
. If

n>1
the proof of Theorem
2.1
completely follows the steps and
reasonings of the proofs of Theorems
10.1
,
11.1
.

Firstly, the boundedness of operator
\emph{L}
in the spaces of Theorem

2.1
follows directly from property (
2.6
) and Proposition

6.3
.

Further, to demonstrate the inverse bounded operator for
\emph{L}
, we,
similar to Theorem
10.1
, assume first that the smoothness exponent

n\alpha\in(0,1)
and it is sufficiently small.

On the first step we reduce the problem to zero initial data (
1.3
). For
that we make use of Theorem
13.3
.

Taking advantage of the fact that the given initial data are equal to zero, we
on the second step extend the desired solution and the right hand side to the
domain
\{t<0\}\cup\{t>T\}
to a finite in
t
function and formulate the
original problem in the domain
R^{N}\times(-\infty,\infty)
. At that equation
(
1.2
) stays unchanged since the derivative in
t
of order
n-1
of the
solution is continuous at
t=0
and consequently no terms of the kind of
supported at
\{t=0\}
distributions emerge in the equation while calculating
the highest
t
-derivative of order
n
- completely similar to the case

n=1
. We also cut the right hand side off to a finite in
x
function and
apply the smoothing to obtain the right hand side from
C^{\infty}(R^{N}\times(-\infty,\infty))
, exactly as it was done in sections
9.4
and

10.2
.

On the next step we obtain solvability and estimates of the solution to the
problem with a finite right hand side of the class
C^{\infty}(R^{N}\times(-\infty,\infty))
. As it was in section
10.3
, we apply the
Fourier transform with respect to all variables and obtain the representation
for the highest derivatives of the solution

\frac{\widehat{\partial^{n}u_{m,\varepsilon}}}{\partial t^{n}}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\frac{(i\xi_{0})^{n}}{(i\xi_{0})^{n}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})\equiv\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0}),

(14.1)

\widehat{(-\Delta_{z_{i}})^{\frac{\sigma_{i}}{2}}u_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\frac{|\zeta_{i}|^{\sigma_{i}}}{(i\xi_{0})^{n}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})\equiv\widehat{m}_{i}(\xi,\xi_{0})\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0}).

(14.2)

Note that the right hand side
f(x,t)
in the extended to the domain
\{t<0\}

problem is not continuous at
t=0
in general since the derivative in
t
of
order
n
in the equation is not generally continuous at
t=0
.
Correspondingly, the smoothed right hand side
f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
in
(
14.1
) and in (
14.2
), similar to section
10.3
, does not have
uniformly bounded in mollifying parameter
\varepsilon
Hölder seminorm in

t
. Consequently, similar to section
10.3
, we apply to the multipliers

\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})
and
\widehat{m}_{i}(\xi,\xi_{0})
not only
Theorem
8.1
to obtain estimate (
10.26
), but also Theorem

8.2
, which permits to obtain uniform in
\varepsilon
estimate of the
highest Hölder seminorms of the solution in the space variables
x
. At
that, since by assumption
n
is not equal to a number of the form
4j+2
,

j=0,1,...
, then
(i\xi_{0})^{n}
is not equal to
-|\xi_{0}|^{n}
for all
nonzero
\xi_{0}
. Consequently, the modulus of denominator in the expressions
for
\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})
and
\widehat{m}_{i}(\xi,\xi_{0})
is
separated from zero on the sets
B_{\nu}
from (
8.9
). Therefore, similar
to section
10.3
, it is not difficult to verify conditions of theorems

8.1
and
8.2
. Application of these theorems leads, under definite
smallness of the exponent
\alpha
, to the estimate of the highest seminorms
of the solution
u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
for the problem with the smoothed
finite right hand side
f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)
. In particular, for the
highest derivative in
t
, based also on Lemma
10.3
, we obtain the
estimate in the whole space
R^{N+1}

\left\langle\frac{\partial^{n}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}}\right\rangle_{t,R^{N+1}}^{(n\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma})\left\langle f_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right\rangle_{R^{N+1}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,n\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha)\varepsilon^{-n\alpha}|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,n\alpha)},

(14.3)

which is an analog of (
10.26
). And similar to (
10.30
), based also on
the equation, we obtain the uniform in
\varepsilon
estimate in the domain

\overline{R_{T}^{N}}

\left\langle u_{m,\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}+\overline{\sigma}\alpha,n+n\alpha)}\leq C(\alpha,\overline{\sigma},\{N_{k}\})\left|f(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,n\alpha)},\quad m=1,2,...,\varepsilon\in(0,1).

(14.4)

To obtain an analog of (
10.32
) and (
10.33
) we first make use of
(
14.3
) to obtain

\left|\frac{\partial^{n}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}}\right|=\left|\frac{\partial^{n}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}}-\frac{\partial^{n}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,-2\varepsilon)}{\partial t^{n}}\right|\leq

\leq\left\langle u_{m,\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{t,R^{N+1}}^{(n+n\alpha)}|-2\varepsilon|^{n\alpha}\leq C(n,\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,n\alpha)},\quad t\in[-2\varepsilon,0].

(14.5)

And then, analogously to (
10.32
),

\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,0)\right|\leq C(n){\int\limits_{-2\varepsilon}^{0}}\left|\frac{\partial^{n}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}}\right|t^{n-1}dt\leq C(n,\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,\alpha)}\varepsilon^{n}.

(14.6)

Eventually, analogously to the obtaining (
10.33
), we successively obtain
on
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}

\left|\frac{\partial^{n}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}}\right|\leq\left|\frac{\partial^{n}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,0)}{\partial t^{n}}\right|_{R^{N}}^{(0)}+\left\langle\frac{\partial^{n}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{\partial t^{n}}\right\rangle_{t,\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(n\alpha)}t^{n\alpha}\leq

\leq C(n,\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,n\alpha)}\left(1+T^{n\alpha}\right)

and consequently

\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|\leq\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,0)\right|+{C(n)\int\limits_{0}^{t}}\left|\frac{\partial^{n}u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,\tau)}{\partial\tau^{n}}\right|(t-\tau)^{n-1}d\tau\leq

\leq C(n,\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,n\alpha)}(\varepsilon^{n}+t^{n}\left(1+T^{n\alpha}\right)),

that is

\left|u_{m,\varepsilon}(x,t)\right|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(n,\overline{\sigma},\alpha)|f|_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,n\alpha)}(1+T^{n+n\alpha}).

(14.7)

Further, completely similar to section
10.3
and analogously to reasoning
of section
9.7
at the proving of Proposition
9.2
, based on
estimates (
14.4
) and (
14.7
), we make the transition to the
limit on the parameters of smoothing and cutting-off under
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
,
m\rightarrow\infty
on the set
\overline{R_{T}^{N}}
in the
sequence of the problems with smooth finite data. This results in a solution

u(x,t)
of the original problem with the estimates

\left\langle u\right\rangle_{\overline{R_{T}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha),n+n\alpha)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},n,\alpha)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,n\alpha)}+{\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}}|u_{i}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{n}\overline{\sigma})}\right),

(14.8)

\left|u\right|_{\overline{R_{\widetilde{T}}^{N}}}^{(0)}\leq C(\overline{\sigma},\alpha,n)\left(|f|_{\overline{R_{\infty}^{N}}}^{(\overline{\sigma}\alpha,n\alpha)}+{\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}}|u_{i}|_{R^{N}}^{(\overline{\sigma}(1+\alpha)-\frac{i}{n}\overline{\sigma})}\right)\times

(14.9)

\times(1+\widetilde{T}^{n+\alpha})+|u_{0}|_{R^{N}}^{(0)},\quad\widetilde{T}\leq T,

where
T
can be equal to
\infty
if the original function
f(x,t)
was
defined on
R^{N}\times[0,\infty)
- analogously to Proposition

10.5
in section
10.4
.

The uniqueness of the solution is obtained completely similar to section

10.4
by the extension of the solution and applying the Fourier transform
in the space of distributions, which completes the proof of the solvability
and the estimates for problem (
1.2
), (
1.3
) under a sufficiently
small
\alpha>0
.

On the last step, repeating verbatim first reasonings from section
10.5

and then from section
11
, we prove the solvability and the estimates of
problem (
1.2
), (
1.3
) for an arbitrary positive
\alpha
.

Thus, as a result, we obtain the existence and the boundedness of the inverse
operator
\emph{L}^{\emph{-1}}
to the operator
\emph{L}
, which completes
the proof of Theorem
2.1
.

14.2
The proof of Theorem
2.3
.

In the case
\theta\in(0,1)
this theorem, evidently, coincides with Theorem

12.3
. In the case of an arbitrary noninteger
\theta>0
the proof step
by step follows the proofs of Theorems
9.1
and
12.3
with some
minor obvious changes. We will just highlight some of them.

Firstly, the reducing of the problem to zero initial data is made not just by
substraction of the initial function similar to section
9.1
, but by
substraction of a function
w(x,t)
from the appropriate space, which
satisfies the initial conditions. Such function was constructed in Theorem

13.1
for an arbitrary noninteger
\theta>0
with the restriction

\{\theta\}+\theta\alpha>1
on the exponent
\alpha
, and also in Theorem

13.2
for a noninteger
\theta\in(0,2)
and for an arbitrary
\alpha>0
.

Further, after the extension of the unknown function by zero to the domain

\{t<0\}
, in view of zero initial data, the derivatives in
t
up to the
order
[\theta]
stays continuous, and consequently, the same is valid for the
fractional derivative
D_{\ast}^{\theta}u(x,t)
itself. Therefore, after
applying, similar to section
9.5
, the Fourier transform to obtain the
representation of the solution in terms of it’s Fourier image we can use
Theorem
8.1
. At that the representations for the highest derivatives
have the form

\widehat{D_{\ast}^{\theta}u(x,t)u_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\frac{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}}{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})\equiv\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0}),

(14.10)

\widehat{(-\Delta_{z_{i}})^{\frac{\sigma_{i}}{2}}u_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})=\frac{|\zeta_{i}|^{\sigma_{i}}}{(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}+{\sum_{k=1}^{r}}|\zeta_{k}|^{\sigma_{k}}}\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0})\equiv\widehat{m}_{i}(\xi,\xi_{0})\widehat{f_{m,\varepsilon}}(\xi,\xi_{0}).

(14.11)

Since
\theta
is a noninteger, then in the first term of the denominator

(i\xi_{0})^{\theta}=(\pm i)^{\theta}|\xi_{0}|^{\theta}
the numerical
coefficient
(\pm i)^{\theta}
has a non-zero imaginary part. Therefore on the
annulus
B_{\nu}
from (
8.9
) the denominator of the multipliers

\widehat{m}_{0}(\xi,\xi_{0})
and
\widehat{m}_{i}(\xi,\xi_{0})
is strictly
separated from zero. This permits to verify the conditions of Theorem

8.1
- completely similar to section
9.5
.

Highlight, finally, one more very simple alteration in the proof, caused by
the high order of the derivative in
t
. To obtain the corresponding analog of
Proposition
9.5
on the extension of the solution to the whole time
interval
t\in(0,\infty)
one should use the Taylor polynomial of degree

[\theta]
on the role of the function
\widetilde{u}(x,t)
from (
9.77
)
and, correspondingly, to use Lemma
9.4
, which is proved for an
arbitrary noninteger
\theta>0
.

Besides the pointed above simplest alterations all the others steps in the
proof of Theorem
2.3
coincide with the corresponding steps in the
proofs first of Theorem
9.1
and then of Theorem
12.3
, which leads
to the assertion of Theorem
2.3
.
